[#printers,running-title="The Printers to the Reader.",margin-top=-1.81pt]
== The Printers to the Reader.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.factotum,margin-top=8.62pt]
image::temple-factotum-t2.png[T,pdfwidth=45.0pt,align=left]

[.prose,margin-top=-45.80pt]
[.i460]##{nbsp}##He dedication of this work having been
[.i475]##{nbsp}##made by the Authour to the __Divine Ma-__
[.i475]##{nbsp}##__jestie__ onely, how should we now presume
[.i475]##{nbsp}##to interest any mortall man in the patro-
[.i475]##{nbsp}##nage of it? Much lesse think we it meet
to seek the recommendation of the Muses, for that
which himself was confident to have been inspired by
a diviner breath then flows from __Helicon.__ The world
therefore shall receive it in that naked simplicitie, with
which he left it, without any addition either of support
or ornament, more then is included in it self. We leave
it free and unforestalled to every mans judgement, and
to the benefit that he shall finde by perusall. Onely for
the clearing of some passages, we have thought it not
unfit to make the common-Reader privie to some few
particularities of the condition and disposition of the
Person;
endif::[]
ifndef::backend-pdf[]
[.prose]
THe dedication of this work having been made by the Authour to the __Divine Majestie__ onely, how should we now presume to interest any mortall man in the patronage of it? Much lesse think we it meet to seek the recommendation of the Muses, for that which himself was confident to have been inspired by a diviner breath then flows from __Helicon.__ The world therefore shall receive it in that naked simplicitie, with which he left it, without any addition either of support or ornament, more then is included in it self. We leave it free and unforestalled to every mans judgement, and to the benefit that he shall finde by perusall. Onely for the clearing of some passages, we have thought it not unfit to make the common-Reader privie to some few particularities of the condition and disposition of the Person;
endif::[]

[.prose.indent,margin-top=-0.19pt]
Being nobly born, and as eminently endued with
gifts of the minde, and having by industrie and happy
education perfected them to that great height of excel-
lencie, whereof his fellowship of Trinitie Colledge in
Cambridge, and his Orator-ship in the Universitie,
together with that knowledge which the Kings Court
had taken of him, could make relation farre above or-
dinarie. Quitting both his deserts and all the opportu-
nities that he had for worldly preferment, he betook
himself to the Sanctuarie and Temple of God, choo-
sing rather to serve at Gods Altar, then to seek the ho-

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.97pt]
nour[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##¶ 2

endif::[]


<<<

// page 4 (leaf 3)
// The Printers to the Reader, its second page.
[.prose.goes-on,margin-top=-17.35pt]
nour of State-employments. As for those inward en-
forcements to this course (for outward there was none)
which many of these ensuing verses bear witnesse of,
they detract not from the freedome, but adde to the
honour of this resolution in him. As God had enabled
him, so he accounted him meet not onely to be called,
but to be compelled to this service: Wherein his faith-
full discharge was such, as may make him justly a com-
panion to the primitive Saints, and a pattern or more
for the age he lived in.

[.prose.indent,margin-top=1.38pt]
To testifie his independencie upon all others, and
to quicken his diligence in this kinde, he used in his or-
dinarie speech, when he made mention of the blessed
name of our Lord and Saviour Jesus Christ, to adde,
__My Master.__

[.prose.indent,margin-top=0.43pt]
Next God, he loved that which God himself hath
magnified above all things, that is, his Word: so as he
hath been heard to make solemne protestation, that he
would not part with one leaf thereof for the whole
world, if it were offered him in exchange.

[.prose.indent,margin-top=-0.31pt]
His obedience and conformitie to the Church and
the discipline thereof was singularly remarkable.
Though he abounded in private devotions, yet went
he every morning and evening with his familie to the
Church; and by his example, exhortations, and encou-
ragements drew the greater part of his parishioners to
accompanie him dayly in the publick celebration of
Divine Service.

[.prose.indent,margin-top=0.72pt]
As for worldly matters, his love and esteem to them
was so little, as no man can more ambitiously seek,
then he did earnestly endeavour the resignation of an
Ecclesiasticall dignitie, which he was possessour of. But
God permitted not the accomplishment of this desire,
having ordained him his instrument for reedifying of
the Church belonging thereunto, that had layen rui-
nated almost twenty yeares. The reparation whereof,

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.94pt]
having[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]


<<<

// page 5 (leaf 4)
// The Printers to the Reader, its last page: its close, Herbert's motto
// in italic, centred, and a flower under it.
[.prose.goes-on,margin-top=-20.78pt]
having been uneffectually attempted by publick col-
lections, was in the end by his own and some few
others private free-will-offerings succesfully effected.
With the remembrance whereof, as of an especiall
good work, when a friend went about to comfort him
on his death-bed, he made answer, __It is a good work,__
__if it be sprinkled with the bloud of Christ:__ otherwise
then in this respect he could finde nothing to glorie or
comfort himself with, neither in this, nor in any other
thing.

[.prose.indent,margin-top=0.42pt]
And these are but a few of many that might be said,
which we have chosen to premise as a glance to some
parts of the ensuing book, and for an example to the
Reader. We conclude all with his own Motto, with
which he used to conclude all things that might seem
to tend any way to his own honour;

[.motto,margin-top=0.36pt]
__Lesse then the least of Gods mercies.__

[#dedication,running-title="The Dedication.",margin-top=21.96pt]
== ¶ The Dedication.

[.v.dropcap.ded,margin-top=10.39pt]
LOrd, my first fruits present themselves to thee;
Yet not mine neither: for from thee they came,
And must return. Accept of them and me,
And make us strive, who shall sing best thy Name.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Turn their eyes hither, who shall make a gain:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Theirs, who shall hurt themselves or me, refrain.

[.band,margin-top=24.63pt]
image::temple-band-dedication-foot.png[pdfwidth=179.9pt]

[#church-porch,running-title="The Church-porch.",margin-top=5.12pt]
== The Church-porch.

[.subhead,margin-top=7.08pt]
Perirrhanterium.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.factotum,margin-top=6.94pt]
image::temple-factotum-t.png[T,pdfwidth=33.53pt,align=left]

[.v,margin-top=-34.08pt]
[.i338]##{nbsp}##Hou, whose sweet youth and early hopes in-
[.i438]##{nbsp}##hance
[.i374]##{nbsp}##Thy rate and price, and mark thee for a
[.i438]##{nbsp}##treasure;
Hearken unto a Verser, who may chance
Ryme thee to good, and make a bait of pleasure.
[.i105]##{nbsp}##A verse may finde him, who a sermon flies,
[.i105]##{nbsp}##And turn delight into a sacrifice.
endif::[]
ifndef::backend-pdf[]
[.v]
THou, whose sweet youth and early hopes inhance
Thy rate and price, and mark thee for a treasure;
Hearken unto a Verser, who may chance
Ryme thee to good, and make a bait of pleasure.
[.i105]##{nbsp}##A verse may finde him, who a sermon flies,
[.i105]##{nbsp}##And turn delight into a sacrifice.
endif::[]

[.v,margin-top=8.06pt]
Beware of lust: it doth pollute and foul
Whom God in Baptisme washt with his own blood.
It blots thy lesson written in thy soul;
The holy lines cannot be understood.
[.i105]##{nbsp}##How dare those eyes upon a Bible look,
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Much lesse towards God, whose lust is all their book?

[.v,margin-top=8.87pt]
Wholly abstain, or wed. Thy bounteous Lord
Allows thee choise of paths: take no by-wayes;
But gladly welcome what he doth afford;
Not grudging, that thy lust hath bounds and staies.
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Continence hath his joy: weigh both; and so
[.i105]##{nbsp}##If rottennesse have more, let Heaven go.

[.v,margin-top=9.23pt]
If God had laid all common, certainly
Man would have been th'incloser: but since now
God hath impal'd us, on the contrary
Man breaks the fence, and every ground will plough.
[.i105]##{nbsp}##O what were man, might he himself misplace!
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Sure to be crosse he would shift feet and face.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.02pt]
Drink[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1145]##{nbsp}##A

endif::[]

<<<

// page 10 (leaf 9)
[.v,margin-top=2.40pt]
Drink not the third glasse, which thou canst not tame,
When once it is within thee; but before
Mayst rule it, as thou list; and poure the shame,
Which it would poure on thee, upon the floore.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##It is most just to throw that on the ground,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Which would throw me there, if I keep the round.

[.v,margin-top=12.74pt]
He that is drunken, may his mother kill
Bigge with his sister: he hath lost the reins,
Is outlawd by himself: all kinde of ill
Did with his liquour slide into his veins.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The drunkard forfets Man, and doth devest
[.i95]##{nbsp}##All worldly right, save what he hath by beast.

[.v,margin-top=12.22pt]
Shall I, to please anothers wine-sprung minde,
Lose all mine own? God hath giv'n me a measure
Short of his canne, and bodie; must I finde
A pain in that, wherein he findes a pleasure?
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Stay at the third glasse: if thou lose thy hold,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Then thou art modest, and the wine grows bold.

[.v,margin-top=12.85pt]
If reason move not Gallants, quit the room,
(All in a shipwrack shift their severall way)
Let not a common ruine thee intombe:
Be not a beast in courtesie; but stay,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Stay at the third cup, or forgo the place.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Wine above all things doth Gods stamp deface.

[.v,margin-top=12.37pt]
Yet, if thou sinne in wine or wantonnesse,
Boast not thereof; nor make thy shame thy glorie.
Frailtie gets pardon by submissivenesse;
But he that boasts, shuts that out of his storie.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##He makes flat warre with God, and doth defie
[.i95]##{nbsp}##With his poore clod of earth the spacious skie.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Take[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 11 (leaf 10)
[.v,margin-top=2.69pt]
Take not his name, who made thy mouth, in vain:
It gets thee nothing, and hath no excuse.
Lust and wine plead a pleasure, avarice gain:
But the cheap swearer through his open sluce
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Lets his soul runne for nought, as little fearing.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Were I an __Epicure,__ I could bate swearing.

[.v,margin-top=12.96pt]
When thou dost tell anothers jest, therein
Omit the oathes, which true wit cannot need:
Pick out of tales the mirth, but not the sinne.
He pares his apple, that will cleanly feed.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Play not away the vertue of that name,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Which is thy best stake, when griefs make thee [.turn-up]##tame.##

[.v,margin-top=13.04pt]
The cheapest sinnes most dearely punisht are;
Because to shun them also is so cheap:
For we have wit to mark them, and to spare.
O crumble not away thy souls fair heap.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##If thou wilt die, the gates of hell are broad:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Pride and full sinnes have made the way a road.

[.v,margin-top=12.37pt]
Lie not; but let thy heart be true to God,
Thy mouth to it, thy actions to them both:
Cowards tell lies, and those that fear the rod;
The stormie working soul spits lies and froth.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Dare to be true. Nothing can need a ly:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##A fault, which needs it most, grows two thereby.

[.v,margin-top=13.18pt]
Flie idlenesse, which yet thou canst not flie
By dressing, mistressing, and complement.
If those take up thy day, the sunne will crie
Against thee: for his light was onely lent.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##God gave thy soul brave wings; put not those fea[.turn-up]##thers##
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Into a bed, to sleep out all ill weathers.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-0.46pt]
Art[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##A 2

endif::[]

<<<

// page 12 (leaf 11)
[.v,margin-top=3.76pt]
Art thou a Magistrate? then be severe:
If studious; copie fair, what time hath blurr'd;
Redeem truth from his jawes: if souldier,
Chase brave employments with a naked sword
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Throughout the world. Fool not: for all may have,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##If they dare try, a glorious life, or grave.

[.v,margin-top=13.59pt]
O England! full of sinne, but most of sloth;
Spit out thy flegme, and fill thy brest with glorie:
Thy Gentry bleats, as if thy native cloth
Transfus'd a sheepishnesse into thy storie:
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Not that they all are so; but that the most
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Are gone to grasse, and in the pasture lost.

[.v,margin-top=12.70pt]
This losse springs chiefly from our education.
Some till their ground, but let weeds choke their sonne:
Some mark a partridge, never their childes fashion:
Some ship them over, and the thing is done.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Studie this art, make it thy great designe;
[.i90]##{nbsp}##And if Gods image move thee not, let thine.

[.v,margin-top=12.96pt]
Some great estates provide, but doe not breed
A mast'ring minde; so both are lost thereby:
Or els they breed them tender, make them need
All that they leave: this is flat povertie.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##For he, that needs five thousand pound to live,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Is full as poore as he, that needs but five.

[.v,margin-top=12.85pt]
The way to make thy sonne rich, is to fill
His minde with rest, before his trunk with riches:
For wealth without contentment, climbes a hill
To feel those tempests, which fly over ditches.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##But if thy sonne can make ten pound his measure,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Then all thou addest may be call'd his treasure.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
When[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 13 (leaf 12)
[.v,margin-top=2.73pt]
When thou dost purpose ought, (within thy power)
Be sure to doe it, though it be but small:
Constancie knits the bones, and makes us stowre,
When wanton pleasures becken us to thrall.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who breaks his own bond, forfeiteth himself:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##What nature made a ship, he makes a shelf.

[.v,margin-top=12.63pt]
Do all things like a man, not sneakingly:
Think the king sees thee still; for his King does.
Simpring is but a lay-hypocrisie:
Give it a corner, and the clue undoes.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who fears to do ill, sets himself to task:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who fears to do well, sure should wear a mask.

[.v,margin-top=12.40pt]
Look to thy mouth; diseases enter there.
Thou hast two sconses, if thy stomack call;
Carve, or discourse; do not a famine fear.
Who carves is kind to two; who talks, to all.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Look on meat, think it dirt, then eat a bit;
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And say withall, Earth to earth I commit.

[.v,margin-top=12.85pt]
Slight those who say amidst their sickly healths,
Thou liv'st by rule. What doth not so, but man?
Houses are built by rule, and common-wealths.
Entice the trusty sunne, if that you can,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##From his Ecliptick line: becken the skie.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who lives by rule then, keeps good companie.

[.v,margin-top=13.07pt]
Who keeps no guard upon himself, is slack,
And rots to nothing at the next great thaw.
Man is a shop of rules, a well truss'd pack,
Whose every parcell under-writes a law.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Lose not thy self, nor give thy humours way:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##God gave them to thee under lock and key.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=3.75pt]
By[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##A 3

endif::[]

<<<

// page 14 (leaf 13)
[.v,margin-top=2.18pt]
By all means use sometimes to be alone.
Salute thy self: see what thy soul doth wear.
Dare to look in thy chest; for'tis thine own:
And tumble up and down what thou find'st there.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who cannot rest till he good fellows finde,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##He breaks up house, turns out of doores his minde.

[.v,margin-top=12.18pt]
Be thrifty, but not covetous: therefore give
Thy need, thine honour, and thy friend his due.
Never was scraper brave man. Get to live;
Then live, and use it: els, it is not true
[.i95]##{nbsp}##That thou hast gotten. Surely use alone
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Makes money not a contemptible stone.

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
Never exceed thy income. Youth may make
Ev'n with the yeare: but age, if it will hit,
Shoots a bow short, and lessens still his stake,
As the day lessens, and his life with it.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Thy children, kindred, friends upon thee call;
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Before thy journey fairly part with all.

[.v,margin-top=12.89pt]
Yet in thy thriving still misdoubt some evil;
Lest gaining gain on thee, and make thee dimme
To all things els. Wealth is the conjurers devil;
Whom when he thinks he hath, the devil hath him.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Gold thou mayst safely touch, but if it stick
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Unto thy hands, it woundeth to the quick.

[.v,margin-top=12.81pt]
What skills it, if a bag of stones or gold
About thy neck do drown thee? raise thy head;
Take starres for money; starres not to be told
By any art, yet to be purchased.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##None is so wastefull as the scraping dame.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##She loseth three for one; her soul, rest, fame.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
By[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 15 (leaf 14)
[.v,margin-top=2.18pt]
By no means runne in debt: take thine own measure.
Who cannot live on twentie pound a yeare,
Cannot on fourtie: he's a man of pleasure,
A kinde of thing that's for it self too deere.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The curious unthrift makes his cloth too wide,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And spares himself, but would his taylor chide.

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
Spend not on hopes. They that by pleading clothes
Do fortunes seek, when worth and service fail,
Would have their tale beleeved for their oathes,
And are like empty vessels under sail.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Old courtiers know this; therefore set out so,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##As all the day thou mayst hold out to go.

[.v,margin-top=12.11pt]
In clothes, cheap handsomnesse doth bear the bell.
Wisedome's a trimmer thing, then shop e're gave.
Say not then, This with that lace will do well;
But, This with my discretion will be brave.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Much curiousnesse is a perpetuall wooing
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Nothing with labour; folly long a doing.

[.v,margin-top=12.33pt]
Play not for gain, but sport. Who playes for more,
Then he can lose with pleasure, stakes his heart;
Perhaps his wives too, and whom she hath bore:
Servants and churches also play their part.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Onely a herauld, who that way doth passe,
Findes his crackt name at length in the church-glasse.

[.v,margin-top=12.63pt]
If yet thou love game at so deere a rate,
Learn this, that hath old gamesters deerely cost:
Dost lose? rise up: dost winne? rise in that state.
Who strive to sit out losing hands, are lost.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Game is a civil gunpowder, in peace
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Blowing up houses with their whole increase.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
In[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##A 4

endif::[]

<<<

// page 16 (leaf 15)
[.v,margin-top=2.77pt]
In conversation boldnesse now bears sway.
But know, that nothing can so foolish be,
As empty boldnesse: therefore first assay
To stuffe thy minde with solid braverie;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Then march on gallant: get substantiall worth.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Boldnesse guilds finely, and will set it forth.

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
Be sweet to all. Is thy complexion sowre?
Then keep such companie; make them thy allay:
Get a sharp wife, a servant that will lowre.
A stumbler stumbles least in rugged way.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Command thy self in chief. He lifes warre knows,
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Whom all his passions follow, as he goes.

[.v,margin-top=12.77pt]
Catch not at quarrels. He that dares not speak
Plainly and home, is coward of the two.
Think not thy fame at ev'ry twitch will break:
By great deeds shew, that thou canst little do;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And do them not: that shall thy wisdome be;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And change thy temperance into braverie.

[.v,margin-top=12.52pt]
If that thy fame with ev'ry toy be pos'd,
'Tis a thinne webbe, which poysonous fancies make;
But the great souldiers honour was compos'd
Of thicker stuffe, which would endure a shake.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Wisdome picks friends; civilitie playes the rest.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##A toy shunn'd cleanly passeth with the best.

[.v,margin-top=12.81pt]
Laugh not too much: the wittie man laughs least:
For wit is newes onely to ignorance.
Lesse at thine own things laugh; lest in the jest
Thy person share, and the conceit advance.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Make not thy sport, abuses: for the fly
[.i100]##{nbsp}##That feeds on dung, is coloured thereby.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Pick[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 17 (leaf 16)
[.v,margin-top=2.14pt]
Pick out of mirth, like stones out of thy ground,
Profanenesse, filthinesse, abusivenesse.
These are the scumme, with which course wits abound:
The fine may spare these well, yet not go lesse.
All things are bigge with jest: nothing that's plain,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##But may be wittie, if thou hast the vein.

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
Wit's an unruly engine, wildly striking
Sometimes a friend, sometimes the engineer.
Hast thou the knack? pamper it not with liking:
But if thou want it, buy it not too deere.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Many affecting wit beyond their power,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Have got to be a deare fool for an houre.

[.v,margin-top=12.56pt]
A sad wise valour is the brave complexion,
That leads the van, and swallows up the cities.
The gigler is a milk-maid, whom infection,
Or a fir'd beacon frighteth from his ditties.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Then he's the sport: the mirth then in him rests,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##And the sad man is cock of all his jests.

[.v,margin-top=12.44pt]
Towards great persons use respective boldnesse:
That temper gives them theirs, and yet doth take
Nothing from thine: in service, care, or coldnesse
Doth ratably thy fortunes marre or make.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Feed no man in his sinnes: for adulation
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Doth make thee parcell-devil in damnation.

[.v,margin-top=13.25pt]
Envie not greatnesse: for thou mak'st thereby
Thy self the worse, and so the distance greater.
Be not thine own worm: yet such jealousie,
As hurts not others, but may make thee better,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Is a good spurre. Correct thy passions spite;
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Then may the beasts draw thee to happy light.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=3.01pt]
When[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##A 5

endif::[]

<<<

// page 18 (leaf 17)
[.v,margin-top=2.14pt]
When basenesse is exalted, do not bate
The place its honour, for the persons sake.
The shrine is that which thou dost venerate;
And not the beast, that bears it on his back.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##I care not though the cloth of state should be
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Not of rich arras, but mean tapestrie.

[.v,margin-top=12.81pt]
Thy friend put in thy bosome: wear his eies
Still in thy heart, that he may see what's there.
If cause require, thou art his sacrifice;
Thy drops of bloud must pay down all his fear:
[.i100]##{nbsp}##But love is lost; the way of friendship's gone,
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Though __David__ had his __Ionathan, Christ__ his __Iohn.__

[.v,margin-top=12.81pt]
Yet be not surety, if thou be a father.
Love is a personall debt. I cannot give
My childrens right, nor ought he take it: rather
Both friends should die, then hinder them to live.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Fathers first enter bonds to natures ends;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And are her sureties, ere they are a friends.

[.v,margin-top=12.45pt]
If thou be single, all thy goods and ground
Submit to love; but yet not more then all.
Give one estate, as one life. None is bound
To work for two, who brought himself to thrall.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##God made me one man; love makes me no more,
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Till labour come, and make my weaknesse score.

[.v,margin-top=13.33pt]
In thy discourse, if thou desire to please:
All such is courteous, usefull, new, or wittie.
Usefulnesse comes by labour, wit by ease;
Courtesie grows in court; news in the citie.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Get a good stock of these, then draw the card;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##That suites him best, of whom thy speech is heard.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=2.19pt]
Entice[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 19 (leaf 18)
[.v,margin-top=1.74pt]
Entice all neatly to what they know best;
For so thou dost thy self and him a pleasure:
(But a proud ignorance will lose his rest,
Rather then shew his cards) steal from his treasure
[.i95]##{nbsp}##What to ask further. Doubts well rais'd do lock
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The speaker to thee, and preserve thy stock.

[.v,margin-top=12.66pt]
If thou be Master-gunner, spend not all
That thou canst speak, at once; but husband it,
And give men turns of speech: do not forestall
By lavishnesse thine own, and others wit,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##As if thou mad'st thy will. A civil guest
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Will no more talk all, then eat all the feast.

[.v,margin-top=12.55pt]
Be calm in arguing: for fiercenesse makes
Errour a fault, and truth discourtesie.
Why should I feel another mans mistakes
More, then his sicknesses or povertie?
[.i95]##{nbsp}##In love I should: but anger is not love,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Nor wisdome neither: therefore gently move:

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
Calmnesse is great advantage: he that lets
Another chafe, may warm him at his fire:
Mark all his wandrings, and enjoy his frets;
As cunning fencers suffer heat to tire.
Truth dwells not in the clouds: the bow that's there,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Doth often aim at, never hit the sphere.

[.v,margin-top=12.52pt]
Mark what another sayes: for many are
Full of themselves, and answer their own notion.
Take all into thee; then with equall care
Ballance each dramme of reason, like a potion.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##If truth be with thy friend, be with them both:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Share in the conquest, and confesse a troth.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Be[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 20 (leaf 19)
[.v,margin-top=2.44pt]
Be usefull where thou livest, that they may
Both want, and wish thy pleasing presence still.
Kindnesse, good parts, great places are the way
To compasse this. Finde out mens wants and will,
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And meet them there. All worldly joyes go lesse
[.i100]##{nbsp}##To the one joy of doing kindnesses.

[.v,margin-top=13.18pt]
Pitch thy behaviour low, thy projects high;
So shalt thou humble and magnanimous be:
Sink not in spirit: who aimeth at the sky,
Shoots higher much then he that means a tree.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##A grain of glorie mixt with humblenesse
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Cures both a fever and lethargicknesse.

[.v,margin-top=13.18pt]
Let thy minde still be bent, still plotting where,
And when, and how the businesse may be done.
Slacknesse breeds worms; but the sure traveller,
Though he alight sometimes, still goeth on.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Active and stirring spirits live alone.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Write on the others, Here lies such a one.

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
Slight not the smallest losse, whether it be
In love or honour: take account of all;
Shine like the sunne in every corner: see
Whether thy stock of credit swell, or fall.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Who say, I care not, those I give for lost;
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And to instruct them, 'twill not quit the cost.

[.v,margin-top=13.77pt]
Scorn no mans love, though of a mean degree;
(Love is a present for a mightie king)
Much lesse make any one thine enemie.
As gunnes destroy, so may a little sling.
[.i100]##{nbsp}##The cunning workman never doth refuse
[.i100]##{nbsp}##The meanest tool, that he may chance to use.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
All[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 21 (leaf 20)
[.v,margin-top=2.29pt]
All forrain wisdome doth amount to this,
To take all that is given; whether wealth,
Or love, or language; nothing comes amisse:
A good digestion turneth all to health:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And then as farre as fair behaviour may,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Strike off all scores; none are so cleare as they.

[.v,margin-top=12.88pt]
Keep all thy native good, and naturalize
All forrain of that name; but scorn their ill:
Embrace their activenesse, not vanities.
Who follows all things, forfeiteth his will.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##If thou observest strangers in each fit,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##In time they'l runne thee out of all thy wit.

[.v,margin-top=12.52pt]
Affect in things about thee cleanlinesse,
That all may gladly board thee, as a flowre.
Slovens take up their stock of noisomnesse
Beforehand, and anticipate their last houre.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Let thy mindes sweetnesse have his operation
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Upon thy body, clothes, and habitation.

[.v,margin-top=12.66pt]
In Almes regard thy means, and others merit.
Think heav'n a better bargain, then to give
Onely thy single market-money for it.
Joyn hands with God to make a man to live.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Give to all something; to a good poore man,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Till thou change names, and be where he began.

[.v,margin-top=13.41pt]
Man is Gods image; but a poore man is
Christs stamp to boot: both images regard.
God reckons for him, counts the favour his:
Write, So much giv'n to God; thou shalt be heard.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Let thy almes go before, and keep heav'ns gate
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Open for thee; or both may come too late.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Restore[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 22 (leaf 21)
[.v,margin-top=-7.00pt]
Restore to God his due in tithe and time:
A tithe purloin'd cankers the whole estate.
Sundaies observe: think when the bells do chime,
'Tis angels musick; therefore come not late.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##God then deals blessings: If a king did so,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who would not haste, nay give, to see the show?

[.v,margin-top=21.99pt]
Twice on the day his due is understood;
For all the week thy food so oft he gave thee.
Thy cheere is mended; bate not of the food,
Because 'tis better, and perhaps may save thee.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Thwart not th' Almighty God: O be not crosse.
Fast when thou wilt; but then 'tis gain, not losse.

[.v,margin-top=12.92pt]
Though private prayer be a brave designe,
Yet publick hath more promises, more love:
And love's a weight to hearts, to eies a signe.
We all are but cold suitours; let us move
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Where it is warmest. Leave thy six and seven;
Pray with the most: for where most pray, is heaven.

[.v,margin-top=12.52pt]
When once thy foot enters the church, be bare.
[.i40]##{nbsp}##God is more there, then thou: for thou art there
Onely by his permission. Then beware,
And make thy self all reverence and fear.
Kneeling ne're spoil'd silk stocking: quit thy state.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##All equall are within the churches gate.

[.v,margin-top=11.48pt]
Resort to sermons, but to prayers most:
Praying 's the end of preaching. O be drest;
Stay not for th' other pin: why thou hast lost
A joy for it worth worlds. Thus hell doth jest
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Away thy blessings, and extreamly flout thee,
Thy clothes being fast, but thy soul loose about thee.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
In[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 23 (leaf 22)
[.v,margin-top=2.58pt]
In time of service seal up both thine eies,
And send them to thine heart; that spying sinne,
They may weep out the stains by them did rise:
Those doores being shut, all by the eare comes in.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who marks in church-time others symmetrie,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Makes all their beautie his deformitie.

[.v,margin-top=12.74pt]
Let vain or busie thoughts have there no part:
Bring not thy plough, thy plots, thy pleasures thither.
Christ purg'd his temple; so must thou thy heart.
All worldly thoughts are but theeves met together
[.i95]##{nbsp}##To couzin thee. Look to thy actions well:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##For churches are either our heav'n or hell.

[.v,margin-top=13.33pt]
Judge not the preacher; for he is thy Judge:
If thou mislike him, thou conceiv'st him not.
God calleth preaching folly. Do not grudge
To pick out treasures from an earthen pot.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The worst speak something good: if all want sense,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##God takes a text, and preacheth patience.

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
He that gets patience, and the blessing which
Preachers conclude with, hath not lost his pains.
He that by being at church escapes the ditch,
Which he might fall in by companions, gains.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##He that loves Gods abode, and to combine
With saints on earth, shall one day with them shine.

[.v,margin-top=13.36pt]
Jest not at preachers language, or expression:
How know'st thou, but thy sinnes made him miscarrie?
Then turn thy faults and his into confession:
God sent him, whatsoe're he be: O tarry,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And love him for his Master: his condition,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Though it be ill, makes him no ill Physician.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
None[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 24 (leaf 23)
[.v,margin-top=0.08pt]
None shall in hell such bitter pangs endure,
As those, who mock at Gods way of salvation.
Whom oyl and balsames kill, what salve can cure?
They drink with greedinesse a full damnation.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The Jews refused thunder; and we, folly.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Though God do hedge us in, yet who is holy?

[.v,margin-top=12.81pt]
Summe up at night, what thou hast done by day;
And in the morning, what thou hast to do.
Dresse and undresse thy soul: mark the decay
And growth of it: if with thy watch, that too
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Be down, then winde up both; since we shall be
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Most surely judg'd, make thy accounts agree.

[.v,margin-top=13.62pt]
In brief, acquit thee bravely; play the man.
Look not on pleasures as they come, but go.
Deferre not the least vertue: lifes poore span
Make not an ell, by trifling in thy wo.
[.i95]##{nbsp}##If thou do ill; the joy fades, not the pains:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##If well; the pain doth fade, the joy remains.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=24.37pt]
image::temple-flowers-porch.png[pdfwidth=105.0pt]
endif::[]

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-94.08pt]
Super-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 25 (leaf 24)
[%notitle,#church,running-title="The Church."]
== The Church

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=8.86pt]
image::temple-band-superliminare.png[pdfwidth=189.4pt]
endif::[]

[.opener,margin-top=27.14pt]
=== Superliminare.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=27.94pt]
THou, whom the former precepts have
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Sprinkled and taught, how to behave
Thy self in church; approach, and taste
The churches mysticall repast.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=8.70pt]
'''

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=9.14pt]
AVoid profanenesse; come not here:
Nothing but holy, pure, and cleare,
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Or that which groneth to be so,
[.i320]##{nbsp}##May at his perill further go.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=35.00pt]
image::temple-band-superliminare-foot.png[pdfwidth=192.1pt]
endif::[]

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=25.61pt]
The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 26 (leaf 25)
// The Altar as the printing sets it: between two bands of ornament, its
// lines as wide apart as the printing's, each part of the altar's shape a
// paragraph as wide as the printing's, each of its lines justified to
// fill it; its words in capitals spaced.
ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=6.50pt]
image::temple-band-altar.png[pdfwidth=184.5pt]
endif::[]

[#the-altar,margin-top=15.63pt]
=== The Altar.

[.altar.altar-1,margin-top=8.66pt]
A broken [.spaced-cap]##A##[.spaced]##LTAR##, Lord, thy servant reares;
Made of a heart, and cemented with teares:

[.altar.altar-2,margin-top=-0.28pt]
Whose parts are as thy hand did frame;
No workmans tool hath touch'd the same.

[.altar.altar-3,margin-top=0.30pt]
A [.spaced-cap]##H##[.spaced]##EART## alone
Is such a stone,
As nothing but
Thy power doth cut.
Wherefore each part
Of my hard heart
Meets in this frame,
To praise thy name:

[.altar.altar-4,margin-top=-0.69pt]
That if I chance to hold my peace,
These stones to praise thee may not cease.

[.altar.altar-5,margin-top=0.16pt]
O let thy blessed [.spaced-cap]##S##[.spaced]##ACRIFICE## be mine,
And sanctifie this [.spaced-cap]##A##[.spaced]##LTAR## to be thine.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=9.49pt]
image::temple-band-altar-foot.png[pdfwidth=187.4pt]
endif::[]

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=13.02pt]
The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 27 (leaf 26)
[margin-top=3.04pt]
=== The Sacrifice.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=8.01pt]
__OH all ye,__ who passe by, whose eyes and minde
To worldly things are sharp, but to me blinde;
To me, who took eyes that I might you finde:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=6.11pt]
The Princes of my people make a head
Against their Maker: they do wish me dead,
Who cannot wish, except I give them bread:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=6.62pt]
Without me each one, who doth now me brave,
Had to this day been an Egyptian slave.
They use that power against me, which I gave:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=6.41pt]
Mine own Apostle, who the bag did beare,
Though he had all I had, did not forbeare
To sell me also, and to put me there:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=6.73pt]
For thirty pence he did my death devise,
Who at three hundred did the ointment prize,
Not half so sweet as my sweet sacrifice:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=7.03pt]
Therefore my soul melts, and my hearts deare treasure
Drops bloud (the onely beads) my words to measure:
O let this cup passe, if it be thy pleasure:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=6.92pt]
These drops being temper'd with a sinners tears,
A Balsome are for both the Hemispheres:
Curing all wounds, but mine; all, but my fears:
[.i945]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Yet[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 28 (leaf 27)
[.v,margin-top=-3.09pt]
Yet my Disciples sleep: I cannot gain
One houre of watching; but their drowsie brain
Comforts not me, and doth my doctrine stain:
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=5.41pt]
Arise, arise, they come. Look how they runne.
Alas! what haste they make to be undone!
How with their lanterns do they seek the sunne!
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.04pt]
With clubs and staves they seek me, as a thief,
Who am the way of truth, the true relief;
Most true to those, who are my greatest grief:
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.85pt]
__Iudas,__ dost thou betray me with a kisse?
Canst thou finde hell about my lips? and misse
Of life, just at the gates of life and blisse?
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.15pt]
See, they lay hold on me, not with the hands
Of faith, but furie: yet at their commands
I suffer binding, who have loos'd their bands:
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.56pt]
All my Disciples flie; fear puts a barre
Betwixt my friends and me. They leave the starre,
That brought the wise men of the East from farre.
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.52pt]
Then from one ruler to another bound
They leade me; urging, that it was not sound
What I taught: Comments would the text confound:
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.77pt]
The Priest and rulers all false witnesse seek
'Gainst him, who seeks not life, but is the meek
And readie Paschal Lambe of this great week:
[.i965]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Then[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 29 (leaf 28)
[.v,margin-top=-2.87pt]
Then they accuse me of great blasphemie,
That I did thrust into the Deitie,
Who never thought that any robberie:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
Some said, that I the Temple to the floore
In three dayes raz'd, and raised as before.
Why, he that built the world can do much more:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.86pt]
Then they condemne me all with that same breath,
Which I do give them daily, unto death.
Thus __Adam__ my first breathing rendereth:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.48pt]
They binde, and leade me unto __Herod:__ he
Sends me to __Pilate.__ This makes them agree;
But yet their friendship is my enmitie:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.78pt]
__Herod__ and all his bands do set me light,
Who teach all hands to warre, fingers to fight,
And onely am the Lord of hosts and might:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.52pt]
__Herod__ in judgement sits, while I do stand;
Examines me with a censorious hand:
I him obey, who all things else command:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.70pt]
The __Iews__ accuse me with despitefulnesse;
And vying malice with my gentlenesse,
Pick quarrels with their onely happinesse:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.97pt]
I answer nothing, but with patience prove
If stonie hearts will melt with gentle love.
But who does hawk at eagles with a dove?
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
My[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 30 (leaf 29)
[.v,margin-top=-3.31pt]
My silence rather doth augment their crie;
My dove doth back into my bosome flie,
Because the raging waters still are high:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=5.37pt]
Heark how they crie aloud still, __Crucifie:__
__It is not fit he live a day,__ they crie,
Who cannot live lesse then eternally:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.30pt]
__Pilate__ a stranger holdeth off; but they,
Mine own deare people, cry, __Away, away,__
With noises confused frighting the day:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.41pt]
Yet still they shout, and crie, and stop their eares,
Putting my life among their sinnes and fears,
And therefore wish __my bloud on them and theirs:__
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.23pt]
See how spite cankers things. These words aright
Used, and wished, are the whole worlds light:
But hony is their gall, brightnesse their night:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.93pt]
They choose a murderer, and all agree
In him to do themselves a courtesie:
For it was their own cause who killed me:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.37pt]
And a seditious murderer he was:
But I the Prince of peace; peace that doth passe
All understanding, more then heav'n doth glasse:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.52pt]
Why, Cesar is their onely King, not I:
He clave the stonie rock, when they were drie;
But surely not their hearts, as I well trie:
[.i950]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Ah![.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 31 (leaf 30)
[.v,margin-top=-2.94pt]
Ah! how they scourge me! yet my tendernesse
Doubles each lash: and yet their bitternesse
Windes up my grief to a mysteriousnesse:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=5.44pt]
They buffet me, and box me as they list,
Who grasp the earth and heaven with my fist,
And never yet, whom I would punish, miss'd:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.27pt]
Behold, they spit on me in scornfull wise,
Who by my spittle gave the blinde man eies,
Leaving his blindnesse to mine enemies:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.77pt]
My face they cover, though it be divine.
As __Moses__ face was vailed, so is mine,
Lest on their double-dark souls either shine:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.57pt]
Servants and abjects flout me; they are wittie:
Now __prophesie who strikes thee,__ is their dittie.
So they in me denie themselves all pitie:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
And now I am deliver'd unto death,
Which each one calls for so with utmost breath,
That he before me well nigh suffereth:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.19pt]
Weep not, deare friends, since I for both have wept
When all my tears were bloud, the while you slept:
Your tears for your own fortunes should be kept:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.22pt]
The souldiers leade me to the common hall;
There they deride me, they abuse me all:
Yet for twelve heav'nly legions I could call:
[.i930]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.40pt]
Then[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 32 (leaf 31)
[.v,margin-top=-3.98pt]
Then with a scarlet robe they me aray;
Which shews my bloud to be the onely way,
And cordiall left to repair mans decay:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=6.41pt]
Then on my head a crown of thorns I wear:
For these are all the grapes __Sion__ doth bear,
Though I my vine planted and watred there:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.34pt]
So sits the earths great curse in __Adams__ fall
Upon my head: so I remove it all
From th' earth unto my brows, and bear the thrall:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.74pt]
Then with the reed they gave to me before,
They strike my head, the rock from whence all store
Of heav'nly blessings issue evermore:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.33pt]
They bow their knees to me, and cry, __Hail king:__
What ever scoffes or scornfulnesse can bring,
I am the floore, the sink, where they it fling:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
Yet since mans scepters are as frail as reeds,
And thorny all their crowns, bloudie their weeds;
I, who am Truth, turn into truth their deeds:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.45pt]
The souldiers also spit upon that face,
Which Angels did desire to have the grace,
And Prophets once to see, but found no place:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.82pt]
Thus trimmed forth they bring me to the rout,
Who __Crucifie him,__ crie with one strong shout.
God holds his peace at man, and man cries out:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=2.57pt]
They[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 33 (leaf 32)
[.v,margin-top=-3.24pt]
They leade me in once more, and putting then
Mine own clothes on, they leade me out agen.
Whom devils flie, thus is he toss'd of men:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
And now wearie of sport, glad to ingrosse
All spite in one, counting my life their losse,
They carrie me to my most bitter crosse:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.78pt]
My crosse I bear my self, untill I faint:
Then Simon bears it for me by constraint,
The decreed burden of each mortall Saint:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.85pt]
O all ye who passe by, behold and see:
Man stole the fruit, but I must climbe the tree;
The tree of life to all, but onely me:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=4.53pt]
Lo, here I hang, charg'd with a world of sinne,
The greater world o'th' two; for that came in
By words, but this by sorrow I must win:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.22pt]
Such sorrow, as if sinfull man could feel,
Or feel his part, he would not cease to kneel,
Till all were melted, though he were all steel:
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.89pt]
But, __O my God, my God!__ why leav'st thou me,
The sonne, in whom thou dost delight to be?
My God, my God——
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Never was grief like mine.__

[.v,margin-top=5.22pt]
Shame tears my soul, my bodie many a wound;
Sharp nails pierce this, but sharper that confound;
Reproches, which are free, while I am bound.
[.i960]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.58pt]
Now[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1145]##{nbsp}##B

endif::[]

<<<

// page 34 (leaf 33)
[.v,margin-top=-3.39pt]
Now heal thy self, Physician; now come down.
Alas! I did so, when I left my crown
And fathers smile for you, to feel his frown:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief like mine?__

[.v,margin-top=4.79pt]
In healing not my self, there doth consist
All that salvation, which ye now resist;
Your safetie in my sicknesse doth subsist:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.74pt]
Betwixt two theeves I spend my utmost breath,
As he that for some robberie suffereth.
Alas! what have I stollen from you? death:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.44pt]
A king my title is, prefixt on high;
Yet by my subjects am condemn'd to die
A servile death in servile companie:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.45pt]
They gave me vineger mingled with gall,
But more with malice: yet, when they did call,
With Manna, Angels food, I fed them all:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.15pt]
They part my garments, and by lot dispose
My coat, the type of love, which once cur'd those
Who sought for help, never malicious foes:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.11pt]
Nay, after death their spite shall further go;
For they will pierce my side, I full well know;
That as sinne came, so Sacraments might flow:
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Was ever grief, &c.__

[.v,margin-top=5.12pt]
But now I die; now all is finished.
My wo, mans weal: and now I bow my head.
Onely let others say, when I am dead,
[.i955]##{nbsp}##__Never was grief like mine.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.32pt]
¶ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 35 (leaf 34)
[margin-top=6.98pt]
=== ¶ The Thanksgiving.

[.v.dropcap.p954,margin-top=6.61pt]
OH King of grief! (a title strange, yet true,
To thee of all kings onely due)
Oh King of wounds! how shall I grieve for thee,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##Who in all grief preventest me?
Shall I weep bloud? why thou hast wept such store
[.i315]##{nbsp}##That all thy body was one doore.
Shall I be scourged, flouted, boxed, sold?
[.i315]##{nbsp}##'Tis but to tell the tale is told.
My God, my God, why dost thou part from me?
[.i50]##{nbsp}##Was such a grief as cannot be.
Shall I then sing, skipping, thy dolefull storie,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##And side with thy triumphant glorie?
Shall thy strokes be my stroking? thorns, my flower?
[.i315]##{nbsp}##Thy rod, my posie? crosse, my bower?
But how then shall I imitate thee, and
[.i315]##{nbsp}##Copie thy fair, though bloudie hand?
Surely I will revenge me on thy love,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##And trie who shall victorious prove.
If thou dost give me wealth; I will restore
[.i315]##{nbsp}##All back unto thee by the poore.
If thou dost give me honour; men shall see,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##The honour doth belong to thee.
I will not marry; or, if she be mine,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##She and her children shall be thine.
My bosome friend, if he blaspheme thy name,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##I will tear thence his love and fame.
One half of me being gone, the rest I give
[.i315]##{nbsp}##Unto some Chappell, die or live.
As for thy passion —But of that anon,
[.i315]##{nbsp}##When with the other I have done.
For thy predestination I'le contrive,
[.i225]##{nbsp}##That three yeares hence, if I survive,

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.92pt]
I'le[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##B 2

endif::[]

<<<

// page 36 (leaf 35)
[.v,margin-top=-0.84pt]
I'le build a spittle, or mend common wayes,
[.i295]##{nbsp}##But mend mine own without delayes.
Then I will use the works of thy creation,
As if I us'd them but for fashion.
The world and I will quarrell; and the yeare
[.i710]##{nbsp}##Shall not perceive, that I am here.
My musick shall finde thee, and ev'ry string
Shall have his attribute to sing;
That all together may accord in thee,
And prove one God, one harmonie.
If thou shalt give me wit, it shall appeare,
If thou hast giv'n it me, 'tis here.
Nay, I will reade thy book, and never move
Till I have found therein thy love;
Thy art of love, which I'le turn back on thee,
O my deare Saviour, Victorie!
Then for thy passion— I will do for that—
[.i325]##{nbsp}##Alas, my God, I know not what.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=5.99pt]
'''

[margin-top=3.50pt]
=== ¶ The Reprisall.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=7.38pt]
I[.i205]##{nbsp}##Have consider'd it, and finde
There is no dealing with thy mighty passion:
For though I die for thee, I am behinde;
[.i320]##{nbsp}##My sinnes deserve the condemnation.

[.v,margin-top=4.41pt]
[.i320]##{nbsp}##O make me innocent, that I
May give a disentangled state and free:
And yet thy wounds still my attempts defie,
[.i320]##{nbsp}##For by thy death I die for thee.

[.v,margin-top=7.58pt]
[.i320]##{nbsp}##Ah! was it not enough that thou
By thy eternall glorie didst outgo me?
Couldst thou not griefs sad conquests me allow,
[.i320]##{nbsp}##But in all vict'ries overthrow me?

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-0.45pt]
Yet[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 37 (leaf 36)
[.v,margin-top=2.55pt]
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Yet by confession will I come
Into the conquest. Though I can do nought
Against thee, in thee I will overcome
[.i360]##{nbsp}##The man, who once against thee fought.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=15.05pt]
'''

[margin-top=6.97pt]
=== ¶ The Agonie.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=14.12pt]
P[.i80]##{nbsp}##Hilosophers have measur'd mountains,
Fathom'd the depths of seas, of states, and kings,
Walk'd with a staffe to heav'n, and traced fountains:
[.i265]##{nbsp}##But there are two vast, spacious things,
The which to measure it doth more behove:
Yet few there are that sound them; Sinne and Love.

[.v,margin-top=12.66pt]
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Who would know Sinne, let him repair
Unto mount Olivet; there shall he see
A man so wrung with pains, that all his hair,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##His skinne, his garments bloudie be.
Sinne is that presse and vice, which forceth pain
To hunt his cruell food through ev'ry vein.

[.v,margin-top=12.82pt]
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Who knows not Love, let him assay
And taste that juice, which on the crosse a pike
Did set again abroach; then let him say
[.i265]##{nbsp}##If ever he did taste the like.
Love is that liquour sweet and most divine,
Which my God feels as bloud; but I, as wine.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##B 3

endif::[]

<<<

// page 38 (leaf 37)
[margin-top=3.52pt]
=== ¶ The Sinner.

[.v.dropcap.p1006,margin-top=6.05pt]
LOrd, how I am all ague, when I seek
What I have treasur'd in my memorie!
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Since, if my soul make even with the week,
Each seventh note by right is due to thee.
I finde there quarries of pil'd vanities,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##But shreds of holinesse, that dare not venture
To shew their face, since crosse to thy decrees
There the circumference earth is, heav'n the centre.
In so much dregs the quintessence is small:
[.i245]##{nbsp}##The spirit and good extract of my heart
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Comes to about the many hundredth part.
Yet Lord restore thine image, heare my call:
[.i245]##{nbsp}##And though my hard heart scarce to thee can [.turn-up]##grone,##
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Remember that thou once didst write in stone.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=8.71pt]
'''

[margin-top=3.23pt]
=== ¶ Good Friday.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.65pt]
O[.i190]##{nbsp}##My chief good,
How shall I measure out thy bloud?
How shall I count what thee befell,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##And each grief tell?

[.v,margin-top=7.25pt]
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Shall I thy woes
Number according to thy foes?
Or, since one starre show'd thy first breath,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Shall all thy death?

[.v,margin-top=7.18pt]
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Or shall each leaf,
Which falls in Autumne, score a grief?
Or cannot leaves, but fruit, be signe
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Of the true vine?

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.29pt]
Then[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 39 (leaf 38)
[.v,margin-top=-0.95pt]
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Then let each houre
Of my whole life one grief devoure;
That thy distresse through all may runne,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##And be my sunne.

[.v,margin-top=6.80pt]
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Or rather let
My severall sinnes their sorrows get;
That as each beast his cure doth know,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Each sinne may so.

[.v,margin-top=7.07pt]
Since bloud is fittest, Lord, to write
Thy sorrows in, and bloudie sight;
My heart hath store, write there, where in
One box doth lie both ink and sinne:

[.v,margin-top=6.92pt]
That when sinne spies so many foes,
Thy whips, thy nails, thy wounds, thy woes,
All come to lodge there, sinne may say,
__No room for me,__ and flie away.

[.v,margin-top=6.48pt]
Sinne being gone, oh fill the place,
And keep possession with thy grace;
Lest sinne take courage and return,
And all the writings blot or burn.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.36pt]
'''

[margin-top=0.96pt]
=== ¶ Redemption.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=3.54pt]
HAving been tenant long to a rich Lord,
[.i75]##{nbsp}##Not thriving, I resolved to be bold,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##And make a suit unto him, to afford
A new small-rented lease, and cancell th' old.

[.v,margin-top=7.29pt]
In heaven at his manour I him sought:
[.i265]##{nbsp}##They told me there, that he was lately gone
[.i265]##{nbsp}##About some land, which he had dearly bought
Long since on earth, to take possession.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
I[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##B 4

endif::[]

<<<

// page 40 (leaf 39)
[.v,margin-top=-0.66pt]
I straight return'd, and knowing his great birth,
[.i310]##{nbsp}##Sought him accordingly in great resorts;
In cities, theatres, gardens, parks, and courts:
At length I heard a ragged noise and mirth

[.v,margin-top=-0.16pt]
Of theeves and murderers: there I him espied,
Who straight, __Your suit is granted,__ said, & died.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=1.30pt]
'''

[margin-top=-1.35pt]
=== ¶ Sepulchre.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-1.43pt]
O Blessed bodie! Whither art thou thrown?
No lodging for thee, but a cold hard stone?
So many hearts on earth, and yet not one
[.i960]##{nbsp}##Receive thee?

[.v,margin-top=7.14pt]
Sure there is room within our hearts good store;
For they can lodge transgressions by the score:
Thousands of toyes dwell there, yet out of doore
[.i960]##{nbsp}##They leave thee.

[.v,margin-top=6.99pt]
But that which shews them large, shews them unfit.
What ever sinne did this pure rock commit,
Which holds thee now? Who hath indited it
[.i960]##{nbsp}##Of murder?

[.v,margin-top=8.99pt]
Where our hard hearts have took up stones to brain thee,
And missing this, most falsly did arraigne thee;
Onely these stones in quiet entertain thee,
[.i960]##{nbsp}##And order.

[.v,margin-top=7.76pt]
And as of old, the law by heav'nly art
Was writ in stone; so thou, which also art
The letter of the word, find'st no fit heart
[.i960]##{nbsp}##To hold thee.

[.v,margin-top=7.62pt]
Yet do we still persist as we began,
And so should perish, but that nothing can,
Though it be cold, hard, foul, from loving man
[.i960]##{nbsp}##Withold thee.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Easter.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 41 (leaf 40)
[margin-top=1.35pt]
=== ¶ Easter.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=1.66pt]
RIse heart; thy Lord is risen. Sing his praise
[.i810]##{nbsp}##Without delayes,
Who takes thee by the hand, that thou likewise
[.i995]##{nbsp}##With him mayst rise:
That, as his death calcined thee to dust,
His life may make thee gold, and much more just.

[.v,margin-top=6.85pt]
Awake, my lute, and struggle for thy part
[.i995]##{nbsp}##With all thy art.
The crosse taught all wood to resound his name,
[.i995]##{nbsp}##Who bore the same.
His streched sinews taught all strings, what key
Is best to celebrate this most high day.

[.v,margin-top=6.91pt]
Consort both heart and lute, and twist a song
[.i995]##{nbsp}##Pleasant and long:
Or since all musick is but three parts vied
[.i995]##{nbsp}##And multiplied;
O let thy blessed Spirit bear a part,
And make up our defects with his sweet art.

[.v,margin-top=6.59pt]
I got me flowers to straw thy way;
I got me boughs off many a tree:
But thou wast up by break of day,
And brought'st thy sweets along with thee.

[.v,margin-top=7.32pt]
The Sunne arising in the East,
Though he give light, and th' East perfume;
If they should offer to contest
With thy arising, they presume.

[.v,margin-top=7.36pt]
Can there be any day but this,
Though many sunnes to shine endeavour?
We count three hundred, but we misse:
There is but one, and that one ever.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Easter[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##B 5

endif::[]

<<<

// page 42 (leaf 41)
// head-base: 660
// Easter-wings' first stanza as the printing sets it: the title upright,
// the stanza turned to the right, its lines set in along a curve into
// wings, as far as the printing sets each in.
[#easter-wings,margin-top=10.85pt]
=== ¶ Easter-wings.

[verse.sideways.clockwise.in-flow.wings,margin-top=52.5pt]
____
Lord, who createdst man in wealth and store,
[.i105]##{nbsp}##Though foolishly he lost the same,
[.i195]##{nbsp}##Decaying more and more,
[.i285]##{nbsp}##Till he became
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Most poore:
[.i370]##{nbsp}##With thee
[.i280]##{nbsp}##O let me rise
[.i195]##{nbsp}##As larks, harmoniously,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And sing this day thy victories:
Then shall the fall further the flight in me.
____

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=61.58pt]
¶ Easter-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 43 (leaf 42)
// head-base: 660
[#easter-wings-2,margin-top=11.59pt]
=== ¶ Easter-wings.

[verse.sideways.clockwise.in-flow.wings,margin-top=56.5pt]
____
My tender age in sorrow did beginne:
[.i100]##{nbsp}##And still with sicknesses and shame
[.i195]##{nbsp}##Thou didst so punish sinne,
[.i285]##{nbsp}##That I became
[.i385]##{nbsp}##Most thinne.
[.i400]##{nbsp}##With thee
[.i285]##{nbsp}##Let me combine,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##And feel this day thy victorie:
[.i100]##{nbsp}##For, if I imp my wing on thine,
Affliction shall advance the flight in me.
____

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=64.07pt]
¶ H. Ba-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 44 (leaf 43)
[margin-top=-0.94pt]
=== ¶ H. Baptisme.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-1.02pt]
AS he that sees a dark and shadie grove,
[.i25]##{nbsp}##Stayes not, but looks beyond it on the skie;
[.i345]##{nbsp}##So when I view my sinnes, mine eyes remove
More backward still, and to that water flie,

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
Which is above the heav'ns, whose spring and rent
[.i215]##{nbsp}##Is in my deare Redeemers pierced side.
[.i215]##{nbsp}##O blessed streams! either ye do prevent
And stop our sinnes from growing thick and wide,

[.v,margin-top=5.41pt]
Or else give tears to drown them, as they grow.
[.i215]##{nbsp}##In you Redemption measures all my time,
[.i215]##{nbsp}##And spreads the plaister equall to the crime:
You taught the book of life my name, that so

[.v,margin-top=5.56pt]
[.i215]##{nbsp}##What ever future sinnes should me miscall,
[.i215]##{nbsp}##Your first acquaintance might discredit all.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.88pt]
'''

[margin-top=-2.98pt]
=== ¶ H. Baptisme.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=3.22pt]
S[.i830]##{nbsp}##Ince, Lord, to thee
[.i405]##{nbsp}##A narrow way and little gate
Is all the passage, on my infancie
[.i525]##{nbsp}##Thou didst lay hold, and antedate
[.i1020]##{nbsp}##My faith in me.

[.v,margin-top=5.07pt]
[.i1020]##{nbsp}##O let me still
[.i570]##{nbsp}##Write thee great God, and me a childe:
Let me be soft and supple to thy will,
[.i615]##{nbsp}##Small to my self, to others milde,
[.i1020]##{nbsp}##Behither ill.

[.v,margin-top=5.33pt]
[.i1020]##{nbsp}##Although by stealth
[.i615]##{nbsp}##My flesh get on, yet let her sister
My soul bid nothing, but preserve her wealth;
[.i450]##{nbsp}##The growth of flesh is but a blister;
[.i615]##{nbsp}##Childhood is health.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Nature.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 45 (leaf 44)
[margin-top=2.82pt]
=== ¶ Nature.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=7.42pt]
FUll of rebellion, I would die,
Or fight, or travell, or denie
That thou hast ought to do with me.
[.i935]##{nbsp}##O tame my heart;
[.i720]##{nbsp}##It is thy highest art
To captivate strong holds to thee.

[.v,margin-top=6.91pt]
If thou shalt let this venome lurk,
And in suggestions fume and work,
My soul will turn to bubbles straight,
[.i1015]##{nbsp}##And thence by kinde
[.i755]##{nbsp}##Vanish into a winde,
Making thy workmanship deceit.

[.v,margin-top=6.70pt]
O smooth my rugged heart, and there
Engrave thy rev'rend law and fear;
Or make a new one, since the old
Is saplesse grown,
[.i755]##{nbsp}##And a much fitter stone
To hide my dust, then thee to hold.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=8.42pt]
'''

[margin-top=3.17pt]
=== ¶ Sinne.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.23pt]
LOrd, with what care hast thou begirt us round!
Parents first season us: then schoolmasters
[.i240]##{nbsp}##Deliver us to laws; they send us bound
To rules of reason, holy messengers,

[.v,margin-top=7.95pt]
Pulpits and sundayes, sorrow dogging sinne,
[.i155]##{nbsp}##Afflictions sorted, anguish of all sizes,
[.i85]##{nbsp}##Fine nets and stratagems to catch us in,
Bibles laid open, millions of surprises,

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Blessings[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 46 (leaf 45)
[.v,margin-top=2.44pt]
Blessings beforehand, tyes of gratefulnesse,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The sound of glorie ringing in our eares:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Without, our shame; within, our consciences;
Angels and grace, eternall hopes and fears.

[.v,margin-top=5.04pt]
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Yet all these fences and their whole aray
[.i95]##{nbsp}##One cunning bosome-sinne blows quite away.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.91pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.61pt]
=== ¶ Affliction.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=2.63pt]
WHen first thou didst entice to thee my heart,
[.i545]##{nbsp}##I thought the service brave:
So many joyes I writ down for my part,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Besides what I might have
Out of my stock of naturall delights,
Augmented with thy gracious benefits.

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
I looked on thy furniture so fine,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##And made it fine to me:
Thy glorious houshold-stuffe did me entwine,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##And 'tice me unto thee.
Such starres I counted mine: both heav'n and earth
Payd me my wages in a world of mirth.

[.v,margin-top=5.36pt]
What pleasures could I want, whose King I served?
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Where joyes my fellows were.
Thus argu'd into hopes, my thoughts reserved
[.i815]##{nbsp}##No place for grief or fear.
Therefore my sudden soul caught at the place,
And made her youth and fiercenesse seek thy face.

[.v,margin-top=5.19pt]
At first thou gav'st me milk and sweetnesses;
[.i815]##{nbsp}##I had my wish and way:
My dayes were straw'd with flow'rs and happinesse;
[.i815]##{nbsp}##There was no moneth but May.
But with my yeares sorrow did twist and grow,
And made a partie unawares for wo.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
My[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 47 (leaf 46)
[.v,margin-top=2.44pt]
My flesh began unto my soul in pain,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Sicknesses cleave my bones;
Consuming agues dwell in ev'ry vein,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##And tune my breath to grones.
Sorrow was all my soul; I scarce beleeved,
Till grief did tell me roundly, that I lived.

[.v,margin-top=13.25pt]
When I got health, thou took'st away my life,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##And more; for my friends die:
My mirth and edge was lost; a blunted knife
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Was of more use then I.
Thus thinne and lean without a fence or friend,
I was blown through with ev'ry storm and winde.

[.v,margin-top=12.66pt]
Whereas my birth and spirit rather took
[.i815]##{nbsp}##The way that takes the town;
Thou didst betray me to a lingring book,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##And wrap me in a gown.
I was entangled in the world of strife,
Before I had the power to change my life.

[.v,margin-top=12.56pt]
Yet, for I threatned oft the siege to raise,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Not simpring all mine age,
Thou often didst with Academick praise
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Melt and dissolve my rage.
I took thy sweetned pill, till I came neare;
I could not go away, nor persevere.

[.v,margin-top=13.21pt]
Yet left perchance I should too happie be
[.i815]##{nbsp}##In my unhappinesse,
Turning my purge to food, thou throwest me
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Into more sicknesses.
Thus doth thy power crosse-bias me, not making
Thine own gift good, yet me from my wayes taking.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Now[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 48 (leaf 47)
[.v,margin-top=-0.51pt]
Now I am here, what thou wilt do with me
[.i810]##{nbsp}##None of my books will show
I reade, and sigh, and wish I were a tree;
[.i810]##{nbsp}##For sure then I should grow
To fruit or shade: at least some bird would trust
Her houshold to me, and I should be just.

[.v,margin-top=5.81pt]
Yet, though thou troublest me, I must be meek;
[.i810]##{nbsp}##In weaknesse must be stout.
Well, I will change the service, and go seek
[.i810]##{nbsp}##Some other master out.
Ah my deare God! though I am clean forgot,
Let me not love thee, if I love thee not.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.55pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.42pt]
=== ¶ Repentance.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.14pt]
LOrd, I confesse my sinne is great;
Great is my sinne: Oh! gently treat
With thy quick flow'r, thy moment anie bloom;
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Whose life still pressing
[.i815]##{nbsp}##Is one undressing,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##A steadie aiming at a tombe.

[.v,margin-top=5.22pt]
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Mans age is two houres work, or three:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Each day doth round about us see.
Thus are we to delights: but we are all
[.i815]##{nbsp}##To sorrows old,
[.i815]##{nbsp}##If life be told
[.i95]##{nbsp}##From what life feeleth, Adams fall.

[.v,margin-top=5.73pt]
[.i95]##{nbsp}##O let thy height of mercie then
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Compassionate short-breathed men.
Cut me not off for my most foul transgression:
[.i815]##{nbsp}##I do confesse
[.i815]##{nbsp}##My foolishnesse;
[.i95]##{nbsp}##My God, accept of my confession.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Sweeten[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 49 (leaf 48)
[.v,margin-top=-3.35pt]
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Sweeten at length this bitter bowl,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Which thou hast pour'd into my soul;
Thy wormwood turn to health, windes to fair weather:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##For if thou stay,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##I and this day,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##As we did rise, we die together.

[.v,margin-top=5.11pt]
[.i90]##{nbsp}##When thou for sinne rebukest man,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Forthwith he waxeth wo and wan:
Bitternesse fills our bowels; all our hearts
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Pine, and decay,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##And drop away,
[.i90]##{nbsp}##And carrie with them th' other parts.

[.v,margin-top=5.29pt]
[.i90]##{nbsp}##But thou wilt sinne and grief destroy;
[.i90]##{nbsp}##That so the broken bones may joy,
And tune together in a well-set song,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Full of his praises,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Who dead men raises.
[.i90]##{nbsp}##Fractures well cur'd make us more strong.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.32pt]
'''

[margin-top=3.14pt]
=== ¶ Faith.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=3.06pt]
L[.i40]##{nbsp}##Ord, how couldst thou so much appease
Thy wrath for sinne, as when mans sight was dimme,
And could see little, to regard his ease,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##And bring by Faith all things to him?

[.v,margin-top=5.38pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Hungrie I was, and had no meat:
I did conceit a most delicious feast;
I had it straight, and did as truly eat,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##As ever did a welcome guest.

[.v,margin-top=6.47pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##There is a rare outlandish root,
Which when I could not get, I thought it here:
That apprehension cur'd so well my foot,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##That I can walk to heav'n well neare.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
I[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 50 (leaf 49)
[.v,margin-top=-3.20pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##I owed thousands and much more:
I did beleeve that I did nothing owe,
And liv'd accordingly; my creditor
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Beleeves so too, and lets me go.

[.v,margin-top=4.82pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Faith makes me any thing, or all
That I beleeve is in the sacred storie:
And where sinne placeth me in Adams fall,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Faith sets me higher in his glorie.

[.v,margin-top=5.74pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##If I go lower in the book,
What can be lower then the common manger?
Faith puts me there with him, who sweetly took
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Our flesh and frailtie, death and danger.

[.v,margin-top=4.85pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##If blisse had lien in art or strength,
None but the wise or strong had gained it:
Where now by Faith all arms are of a length;
[.i245]##{nbsp}##One size doth all conditions fit.

[.v,margin-top=5.15pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##A peasant may beleeve as much
As a great Clerk, and reach the highest stature.
Thus dost thou make proud knowledge bend & croud
[.i245]##{nbsp}##While grace fills up uneven nature.

[.v,margin-top=5.23pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##When creatures had no reall light
Inherent in them, thou didst make the sunne,
Impute a lustre, and allow them bright;
[.i245]##{nbsp}##And in this shew, what Christ hath done.

[.v,margin-top=5.19pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##That which before was darkned clean
With bushie groves, pricking the lookers eye,
Vanisht away, when Faith did change the scene:
[.i245]##{nbsp}##And then appear'd a glorious skie.

[.v,margin-top=5.55pt]
[.i245]##{nbsp}##What though my body runne to dust?
Faith cleaves unto it, counting evr'y grain
With an exact and most particular trust,
[.i245]##{nbsp}##Reserving all for flesh again.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.06pt]
¶ Prayer.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 51 (leaf 50)
[margin-top=0.61pt]
=== ¶ Prayer.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.13pt]
PRayer the Churches banquet, Angels age,
[.i80]##{nbsp}##Gods breath in man returning to his birth,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##The soul in paraphrase, heart in pilgrimage,
The Christian plummet sounding heav'n and earth;

[.v,margin-top=5.41pt]
Engine against th' Almightie, sinners towre,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Reversed thunder, Christ-side-piercing spear,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##The six-daies world-transposing in an houre,
A kinde of tune, which all things heare and fear,

[.v,margin-top=5.52pt]
Softnesse, and peace, and joy, and love, and blisse,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Exalted Manna, gladnesse of the best,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Heaven in ordinarie, man well drest,
The milkie way, the bird of Paradise,

[.v,margin-top=8.65pt]
Church-bels beyond the starres heard, the souls bloud,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##The land of spices; something understood.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=5.73pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.96pt]
=== ¶ The H. Communion.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=3.81pt]
NOt in rich furniture, or fine aray,
[.i175]##{nbsp}##Nor in a wedge of gold,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Thou, who from me wast sold,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##To me dost now thy self convey;
For so thou should'st without me still have been,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Leaving within me sinne:

[.v,margin-top=4.89pt]
But by the way of nourishment and strength
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Thou creep'st into my breast;
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Making thy way my rest,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##And thy small quantities my length;
Which spread their forces into every part,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Meeting sinnes force and art.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Yet[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 52 (leaf 51)
[.v,margin-top=-3.39pt]
Yet can these not get over to my soul,
[.i365]##{nbsp}##Leaping the wall that parts
[.i180]##{nbsp}##Our souls and fleshly hearts;
[.i180]##{nbsp}##But as th' outworks, they may controll
My rebel-flesh, and carrying thy name,
[.i100]##{nbsp}##Affright both sinne and shame.

[.v,margin-top=11.49pt]
Onely thy grace, which with these elements comes,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Knoweth the ready way,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##And hath the privie key,
[.i310]##{nbsp}##Op'ning the souls most subtile rooms;
While those to spirits refin'd, at doore attend
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Dispatches from their friend.

[.v,margin-top=20.47pt]
Give me my captive soul, or take
[.i180]##{nbsp}##My bodie also thither.
Another lift like this will make
[.i180]##{nbsp}##Them both to be together.

[.v,margin-top=11.35pt]
Before that sinne turn'd flesh to stone,
[.i180]##{nbsp}##And all our lump to leaven;
A fervent sigh might well have blown
[.i180]##{nbsp}##Our innocent earth to heaven.

[.v,margin-top=10.68pt]
For sure when Adam did not know
[.i180]##{nbsp}##To sinne, or sinne to smother;
He might to heav'n from Paradise go,
[.i180]##{nbsp}##As from one room t'another.

[.v,margin-top=11.82pt]
Thou hast restor'd us to this ease
[.i180]##{nbsp}##By this thy heav'nly bloud;
Which I can go to, when I please,
[.i180]##{nbsp}##And leave th' earth to their food.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.61pt]
¶ Antiphon.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 53 (leaf 52)
[margin-top=6.76pt]
=== ¶ Antiphon.

[.v,margin-top=9.37pt]
__Cho.__ LEt all the world in ev'ry corner sing,
[.i155]##{nbsp}##My God and King.

[.v,margin-top=11.13pt]
[.i435]##{nbsp}##__Vers.__ The heav'ns are not too high,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##His praise may thither flie:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##The earth is not too low,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##His praises there may grow.

[.v,margin-top=9.57pt]
__Cho.__ Let all the world in ev'ry corner sing,
My God and King.

[.v,margin-top=6.11pt]
[.i435]##{nbsp}##__Vers.__ The church with psalms must shout
No doore can keep them out:
But above all, the heart
Must bear the longest part.

[.v,margin-top=8.10pt]
__Cho.__ Let all the world in ev'ry corner sing,
[.i895]##{nbsp}##My God and King.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=10.06pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.95pt]
=== ¶ Love I.

[.v.dropcap]
IMmortall Love, authour of this great frame,
Sprung from that beauty which can never fade;
How hath man parcel'd out thy glorious name,
And thrown it on that dust which thou hast made,

[.v,margin-top=18.06pt]
While mortall love doth all the title gain!
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Which siding with invention, they together
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Bear all the sway, possessing heart and brain,
(Thy workmanship) and give thee share in neither.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Wit[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 54 (leaf 53)
[.v,margin-top=4.06pt]
Wit fancies beautie, beautie raiseth wit:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##The world is theirs; they two play out the game,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Thou standing by: and though thy glorious name
Wrought our deliverance from th' infernall pit,

[.v,margin-top=7.51pt]
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Who sings thy praise? onely a skarf or glove
[.i95]##{nbsp}##Doth warm our hands, and make them write of [.turn-up]##love.##

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.89pt]
'''

[margin-top=0.57pt]
=== II.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.20pt]
IMmortall Heat, O let thy greater flame
[.i390]##{nbsp}##Attract the lesser to it: let those fires,
Which shall consume the world, first make it tame
And kindle in our hearts such true desires,

[.v,margin-top=7.25pt]
As may consume our lusts, and make thee way.
Then shall our hearts pant thee; then shall our brain
[.i95]##{nbsp}##All her invention on thine Altar lay,
And there in hymnes send back thy fire again:

[.v,margin-top=7.18pt]
Our eies shall see thee, which before saw dust;
Dust blown by wit, till that they both were blinde
Thou shalt recover all thy goods in kinde,
Who wert disseized by usurping lust:

[.v,margin-top=6.88pt]
[.i95]##{nbsp}##All knees shall bow to thee; all wits shall rise,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##And praise him who did make and mend our eies.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=7.18pt]
'''
