[.i340]##{nbsp}##And give me up to night?
For many weeks of lingring pain and smart
But one half houre of comfort for my heart?

[.v,margin-top=7.99pt]
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Me thinks delight should have
More skill in musick, and keep better time.
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Wert thou a winde or wave,
They quickly go and come with lesser crime:
Flow'rs look about, and die not in their prime.

[.v,margin-top=7.76pt]
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Thy short abode and stay
Feeds not, but addes to the desire of meat.
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Lime begg'd of old (they say)
A neighbour spring to cool his inward heat;
Which by the springs accesse grew much more great.

[.v,margin-top=7.77pt]
[.i340]##{nbsp}##In hope of thee my heart
Pickt here and there a crumme, and would not die;
[.i340]##{nbsp}##But constant to his part
When as my fears foretold this, did replie,
A slender thread a gentle guest will tie.

[.v,margin-top=7.58pt]
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Yet if the heart that wept
Must let thee go, return when it doth knock.
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Although thy heap be kept
For future times, the droppings of the stock
May oft break forth, and never break the lock.

[.v,margin-top=7.91pt]
[.i340]##{nbsp}##If I have more to spinne,
The wheel shall go, so that thy stay be short.
[.i340]##{nbsp}##Thou knowst how grief and sinne
Disturb the work. O make me not their sport,
Who by thy coming may be made a court!

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Assurance.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 157 (leaf 156)
[margin-top=4.44pt]
=== § Assurance.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.03pt]
O[.i185]##{nbsp}##Spitefull bitter thought!
Bitterly spitefull thought! Couldst thou invent
So high a torture? Is such poyson bought?
Doubtlesse, but in the way of punishment,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##When wit contrives to meet with thee,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##No such rank poyson can there be.

[.v,margin-top=8.13pt]
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Thou said'st but even now,
That all was not so fair, as I conceiv'd,
Betwixt my God and me; that I allow
And coin large hopes; but, that I was deceiv'd:
[.i190]##{nbsp}##Either the league was broke, or neare it;
[.i190]##{nbsp}##And, that I had great cause to fear it.

[.v,margin-top=8.17pt]
[.i395]##{nbsp}##And what to this? what more
Could poyson, if it had a tongue, expresse?
What is thy aim? wouldst thou unlock the doore
To cold despairs, and gnawing pensivenesse?
[.i190]##{nbsp}##Wouldst thou raise devils? I see, I know,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##I writ thy purpose long ago.

[.v,margin-top=8.09pt]
[.i395]##{nbsp}##But I will to my Father,
Who heard thee say it. O most gracious Lord,
If all hope and comfort that I gather,
Were from my self, I had not half a word,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##Not half a letter to oppose
[.i190]##{nbsp}##What is objected by my foes.

[.v,margin-top=8.25pt]
[.i395]##{nbsp}##But thou art my desert:
And in this league, which now my foes invade,
Thou art not onely to perform thy part,
But also mine; as when the league was made
[.i190]##{nbsp}##Thou didst at once thy self indite,
[.i190]##{nbsp}##And hold my hand, while I did write.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=4.77pt]
Wherefore[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##G 3

endif::[]

<<<

// page 158 (leaf 157)
[.v,margin-top=-0.37pt]
[.i460]##{nbsp}##Wherefore if thou canst fail,
Then can thy truth and I: but while rocks stand,
And rivers stirre, thou canst not shrink or quail:
Yea, when both rocks and all things shall disband,
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Then shalt thou be my rock and tower,
[.i185]##{nbsp}##And make their ruine praise thy power.

[.v,margin-top=12.08pt]
[.i460]##{nbsp}##Now foolish thought go on,
Spin out thy thread, and make thereof a coat
To hide thy shame: for thou hast cast a bone
Which bounds on thee, and will not down thy throat:
[.i185]##{nbsp}##What for it self love once began,
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Now love and truth will end in man.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=12.99pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.91pt]
=== § The Call.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=8.49pt]
COme, my Way, my Truth, my Life:
Such a Way, as gives us breath:
Such a Truth, as ends all strife:
And such a Life, as killeth death.

[.v,margin-top=11.71pt]
Come, my Light, my Feast, my Strength:
Such a Light, as shows a feast:
Such a Feast, as mends in length:
Such a Strength, as makes his guest.

[.v,margin-top=12.30pt]
Come, my Joy, my Love, my Heart:
Such a Joy, as none can move:
Such a Love, as none can part:
Such a Heart, as joyes in love.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Clasping[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 159 (leaf 158)
[margin-top=9.01pt]
=== ¶ Clasping of hands.

[.v.dropcap.p964,margin-top=6.82pt]
LOrd, thou art mine, and I am thine,
If mine I am: and thine much more,
Then I or ought, or can be mine.
Yet to be thine, doth me restore;
So that again I now am mine,
And with advantage mine the more.
Since this being mine, brings with it thine,
And thou with me dost thee restore.
[.i165]##{nbsp}##If I without thee would be mine,
[.i165]##{nbsp}##I neither should be mine nor thine.

[.v.p968,margin-top=11.95pt]
Lord, I am thine, and thou art mine:
So mine thou art, that something more
I may presume thee mine, then thine.
For thou didst suffer to restore
Not thee, but me, and to be mine:
And with advantage mine the more,
Since thou in death wast none of thine,
Yet then as mine didst me restore.
[.i165]##{nbsp}##O be mine still! still make me thine!
[.i165]##{nbsp}##Or rather make no Thine and Mine!

[.poem-rule,margin-top=14.05pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.84pt]
=== ¶ Praise.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.75pt]
LOrd, I will mean and speak thy praise,
Thy praise alone.
My busie heart shall spin it all my dayes:
[.i180]##{nbsp}##And when it stops for want of store,
Then will I wring it with a sigh or grone,
[.i260]##{nbsp}##That thou mayst yet have more.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.95pt]
When[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##G 4

endif::[]

<<<

// page 160 (leaf 159)
[.v,margin-top=4.28pt]
[.i185]##{nbsp}##When thou dost favour any action,
[.i800]##{nbsp}##It runnes, it flies:
All things concurre to give it a perfection.
[.i185]##{nbsp}##That which had but two legs before,
When thou dost blesse, hath twelve: one wheel doth rife
[.i485]##{nbsp}##To twentie then, or more.

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
[.i185]##{nbsp}##But when thou dost on businesse blow,
[.i850]##{nbsp}##It hangs, it clogs:
Not all the teams of Albion in a row
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Can hale or draw it out of doore.
Legs are but stumps, and Pharaohs wheels but logs,
[.i485]##{nbsp}##And struggling hinders more.

[.v,margin-top=12.29pt]
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Thousands of things do thee employ
[.i800]##{nbsp}##In ruling all
This spacious globe: Angels must have their joy,
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Devils their rod, the sea his shore,
The windes their stint: and yet when I did call,
[.i485]##{nbsp}##Thou heardst my call, and more.

[.v,margin-top=12.45pt]
[.i185]##{nbsp}##I have not lost one single tear:
[.i800]##{nbsp}##But when mine eyes
Did weep to heav'n, they found a bottle there
[.i185]##{nbsp}##(As we have boxes for the poore)
Readie to take them in; yet of a size
[.i485]##{nbsp}##That would contain much more.

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
[.i185]##{nbsp}##But after thou hadst slipt a drop
[.i800]##{nbsp}##From thy right eye,
(Which there did hang like streamers neare the top
[.i185]##{nbsp}##Of some fair church to show the sore
And bloudie battell which thou once didst trie)
[.i485]##{nbsp}##The glasse was full and more.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Wherefore[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 161 (leaf 160)
[.v,margin-top=0.08pt]
[.i175]##{nbsp}##Wherefore I sing. Yet since my heart,
[.i835]##{nbsp}##Though press'd, runnes thin;
O that I might some other hearts convert,
[.i175]##{nbsp}##And so take up at use good store:
That to thy chests there might be coming in
[.i835]##{nbsp}##Both all my praise, and more!

[.poem-rule,margin-top=11.22pt]
'''

[margin-top=6.73pt]
=== § Josephs coat.

[.v.dropcap.p966,margin-top=6.51pt]
WOunded I sing, tormented I indite,
Thrown down I fall into a bed, and rest:
Sorrow hath chang'd its note: such is his will,
Who changeth all things, as him pleaseth best.
[.i155]##{nbsp}##For well he knows, if but one grief and smart
Among my many had his full career,
Sure it would carrie with it ev'n my heart,
And both would runne untill they found a biere
[.i155]##{nbsp}##To fetch the bodie; both being due to grief.
But he hath spoil'd the race; and giv'n to anguish
One of Joyes coats, ticing it with relief
To linger in me, and together languish.
[.i155]##{nbsp}##I live to shew his power, who once did bring
[.i155]##{nbsp}##My __joyes__ to __weep,__ and now my __griefs__ to __sing.__

[.poem-rule,margin-top=10.64pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.53pt]
=== ¶ The Pulley.

[.v,margin-top=6.79pt]
[.i295]##{nbsp}##WHen God at first made man,
Having a glasse of blessings standing by;
Let us (said he) poure on him all we can:
Let the worlds riches, which dispersed lie,
[.i750]##{nbsp}##Contract into a span.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
So[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##G 5

endif::[]

<<<

// page 162 (leaf 161)
[.v,margin-top=-0.29pt]
[.i475]##{nbsp}##So strength first made a way;
Then beautie flow'd, then wisdome, honour, pleasure:
When almost all was out, God made a stay,
Perceiving that alone of all his treasure
[.i475]##{nbsp}##Rest in the bottome lay.

[.v,margin-top=11.74pt]
[.i475]##{nbsp}##For if I should (said he)
Bestow this jewell also on my creature,
He would adore my gifts in stead of me,
And rest in Nature, not the God of Nature,
[.i475]##{nbsp}##So both should losers be.

[.v,margin-top=11.27pt]
[.i475]##{nbsp}##Yet let him keep the rest,
But keep them with repining restlesnesse:
Let him be rich and wearie, that at least,
If goodnesse leade him not, yet wearinesse
[.i475]##{nbsp}##May tosse him to my breast.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=10.50pt]
'''

[margin-top=9.93pt]
=== § The Priesthood.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.66pt]
BLest Order, which in power dost so excell,
That with th' one hand thou liftest to the sky,
And with the other throwest down to hell
In thy just censures; fain would I draw nigh,
Fain put thee on, exchanging my lay-sword
[.i585]##{nbsp}##For that of th' holy word.

[.v,margin-top=11.30pt]
But thou art fire, sacred and hallow'd fire;
And I but earth and clay: should I presume
To wear thy habit, the severe attire
My slender compositions might consume.
I am both foul and brittle; much unfit
[.i585]##{nbsp}##To deal in holy Writ.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Yet[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 163 (leaf 162)
[.v,margin-top=-0.22pt]
Yet have I often seen, by cunning hand
And force of fire, what curious things are made
Of wretched earth. Where once I scorn'd to stand,
That earth is fitted by the fire and trade
Of skilfull artists, for the boards of those
[.i585]##{nbsp}##Who make the bravest shows.

[.v,margin-top=12.67pt]
But since those great ones, be they ne're so great,
Come from the earth, from whence those vessels come;
So that at once both feeder, dish, and meat
Have one beginning and one finall summe:
I do not greatly wonder at the sight,
[.i585]##{nbsp}##If earth in earth delight.

[.v,margin-top=11.92pt]
But th' holy men of God such vessels are,
As serve him up, who all the world commands:
When God vouchsafeth to become our fare,
Their hands conucy him, who conveys their hands.
O what pure things, most pure must those things be,
[.i585]##{nbsp}##Who bring my God to me!

[.v,margin-top=11.56pt]
Therefore I dare not, I, put forth my hand
To hold the Ark, although it seem to shake
Through th' old sinnes and new doctrines of our land,
Onely, since God doth often vessels make
Of lowly matter for high uses meet,
[.i585]##{nbsp}##I throw me at his feet.

[.v,margin-top=12.26pt]
There will I lie, untill my Maker seek
For some mean stuffe whereon to show his skill:
Then is my time. The distance of the meek
Doth flatter power. Lest good come short of ill
In praising might, the poore do by submission
[.i585]##{nbsp}##What pride by opposition.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 164 (leaf 163)
[margin-top=3.93pt]
=== ¶ The Search.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.50pt]
WHither, O, whither art thou fled,
[.i415]##{nbsp}##My Lord, my Love?
My searches are my daily bread;
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Yet never prove.

[.v,margin-top=8.02pt]
My knees pierce th'earth, mine eies the skie;
[.i685]##{nbsp}##And yet the sphere
And centre both to me denie
[.i685]##{nbsp}##That thou art there.

[.v,margin-top=7.44pt]
Yet can I mark how herbs below
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Grow green and gay,
As if to meet thee they did know,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##While I decay.

[.v,margin-top=8.10pt]
Yet can I mark how starres above
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Simper and shine,
As having keyes unto thy love,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##While poore I pine.

[.v,margin-top=7.87pt]
I sent a sigh to seek thee out,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Deep drawn in pain,
Wing'd like an arrow: but my scout
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Returns in vain.

[.v,margin-top=7.73pt]
I tun'd another (having store)
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Into a grone;
Because the search was dumbe before:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##But all was one.

[.v,margin-top=8.25pt]
Lord, dost thou some new fabrick mold
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Which favour winnes,
And keeps the present, leaving th' old
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Unto their sinnes?

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Where[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 165 (leaf 164)
[.v,margin-top=-2.76pt]
Where is my God? what hidden place
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Conceals thee still?
What covert dare eclipse thy face?
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Is it thy will?

[.v,margin-top=6.11pt]
O let not that of any thing;
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Let rather brasse,
Or steel, or mountains be thy ring,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##And I will passe.

[.v,margin-top=6.18pt]
Thy will such an intrenching is,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##As passeth thought:
To it all strength, all subtilties
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Are things of nought.

[.v,margin-top=5.59pt]
Thy will such a strange distance is,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##As that to it
East and West touch, the poles do kisse,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##And parallels meet.

[.v,margin-top=6.22pt]
Since then my grief must be as large,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##As is thy space,
Thy distance from me; see my charge,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Lord, see my case.

[.v,margin-top=6.00pt]
O take these barres, these lengths away;
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Turn, and restore me:
Be not Almightie, let me say,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Against, but for me.

[.v,margin-top=5.96pt]
When thou dost turn, and wilt be neare;
[.i670]##{nbsp}##What edge so keen,
What point so piercing can appeare
[.i670]##{nbsp}##To come between?

[.v,margin-top=6.26pt]
For as thy absence doth excell
[.i670]##{nbsp}##All distance known:
So doth thy nearenesse bear the bell,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Making two one.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.14pt]
¶ Grief.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 166 (leaf 165)
[margin-top=8.13pt]
=== ¶ Grief.

[.v.dropcap.p968,margin-top=9.96pt]
O Who will give me tears? Come all ye springs,
Dwell in my head and eyes: come clouds, & rain:
My grief hath need of all the watry things,
That nature hath produc'd. Let ev'ry vein
Suck up a river to supply mine eyes,
My weary weeping eyes too drie for me,
Unlesse they get new conduits, new supplies
To bear them out, and with my state agree.
What are two shallow foords, two little spouts
Of a lesse world? the greater is but small,
A narrow cupboard for my griefs and doubts,
Which want provision in the midst of all.
Verses, ye are too fine a thing, too wise
For my rough sorrows: cease, be dumbe and mute,
Give up your feet and running to mine eyes,
And keep your measures for some lovers lute,
Whose grief allows him musick and a ryme:
For mine excludes both measure, tune, and time.
Alas, my God!

[.poem-rule,margin-top=23.96pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.83pt]
=== ¶ The Crosse.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.64pt]
WHat is this strange and uncouth thing?
To make me sigh, and seek, and faint, and die,
Untill I had some place, where I might sing,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##And serve thee; and not onely I,
But all my wealth, and familie might combine
To set thy honour up, as our designe.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
And[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 167 (leaf 166)
[.v,margin-top=-0.51pt]
[.i270]##{nbsp}##And then when after much delay,
Much wrastling, many a combate, this deare end,
So much desir'd, is giv'n, to take away
[.i270]##{nbsp}##My power to serve thee; to unbend
All my abilities, my designes confound,
And lay my threatnings bleeding on the ground.

[.v,margin-top=12.59pt]
[.i270]##{nbsp}##One ague dwelleth in my bones,
Another in my soul (the memorie
What I would do for thee, if once my grones
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Could be allow'd for harmonie)
I am in all a weak disabled thing,
Save in the sight thereof, where strength doth sting.

[.v,margin-top=11.63pt]
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Besides, things sort not to my will,
Ev'n when my will doth studie thy renown:
Thou turnest th' edge of all things on me still,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Taking me up to throw me down:
So that, ev'n when my hopes seem to be sped,
I am to grief alive, to them as dead.

[.v,margin-top=11.74pt]
[.i270]##{nbsp}##To have my aim, and yet to be
Farther from it then when I bent my bow;
To make my hopes my torture, and the fee
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Of all my woes another wo,
Is in the midst of delicates to need,
And ev'n in Paradise to be a weed.

[.v,margin-top=11.89pt]
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Ah my deare Father, ease my smart!
These contrarieties crush me: these crosse actions
Doe winde a rope about, and cut my heart:
[.i270]##{nbsp}##And yet since these thy contradictions
Are properly a crosse felt by thy sonne,
With but foure words, my words, __Thy will be done.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
§ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 168 (leaf 167)
[margin-top=5.58pt]
=== ¶ The Flower.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=12.80pt]
H[.i145]##{nbsp}##Ow fresh, O Lord, how sweet and clean
Are thy returns! ev'n as the flow'rs in spring;
[.i330]##{nbsp}##To which, besides their own demean,
The late-past frosts tributes of pleasure bring.
[.i980]##{nbsp}##Grief melts away
[.i980]##{nbsp}##Like snow in May,
[.i330]##{nbsp}##As if there were no such cold thing.

[.v,margin-top=16.09pt]
[.i330]##{nbsp}##Who would have thought my shrivel'd heart
Could have recover'd greennesse? It was gone
[.i330]##{nbsp}##Quite under ground, as flow'rs depart
To see their mother-root, when they have blown;
[.i980]##{nbsp}##Where they together
[.i980]##{nbsp}##All the hard weather,
[.i330]##{nbsp}##Dead to the world, keep house unknown.

[.v,margin-top=15.57pt]
[.i330]##{nbsp}##These are thy wonders, Lord of power,
Killing and quickning, bringing down to hell
[.i330]##{nbsp}##And up to heaven in an houre;
Making a chiming of a passing-bell,
[.i980]##{nbsp}##We say amisse,
[.i980]##{nbsp}##This or that is:
[.i330]##{nbsp}##Thy word is all, if we could spell.

[.v,margin-top=12.63pt]
[.i330]##{nbsp}##O that I once past changing were,
Fast in thy Paradise, where no flow'r can wither!
[.i330]##{nbsp}##Many a spring I shoot up fair,
Offring at heav'n, growing and groning thither:
[.i980]##{nbsp}##Nor doth my flower
[.i980]##{nbsp}##Want a spring-showre,
[.i330]##{nbsp}##My sinnes and I joining together:

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
But[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 169 (leaf 168)
[.v,margin-top=-0.14pt]
[.i200]##{nbsp}##But while I grow in a straight line,
Still upwards bent, as if heav'n were mine own,
[.i200]##{nbsp}##Thy anger comes, and I decline:
What frost to that? what pole is not the zone,
[.i825]##{nbsp}##Where all things burn,
[.i825]##{nbsp}##When thou dost turn,
[.i200]##{nbsp}##And the least frown of thine is shown?

[.v,margin-top=6.80pt]
[.i200]##{nbsp}##And now in age I bud again,
After so many deaths I live and write;
[.i200]##{nbsp}##I once more smell the dew and rain,
And relish versing: O my onely light,
[.i825]##{nbsp}##It cannot be
[.i825]##{nbsp}##That I am he
[.i200]##{nbsp}##On whom thy tempests fell all night.

[.v,margin-top=6.29pt]
[.i200]##{nbsp}##These are thy wonders, Lord of love,
To make us see we are but flow'rs that glide:
[.i200]##{nbsp}##Which when we once can finde and prove,
Thou hast a garden for us, where to bide.
[.i825]##{nbsp}##Who would be more,
[.i825]##{nbsp}##Swelling through store,
[.i200]##{nbsp}##Forfeit their Paradise by their pride.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=3.26pt]
'''

[margin-top=-0.34pt]
=== ¶ Dotage.

[.v.dropcap.p980,margin-top=-1.04pt]
FAlse glozing pleasures, casks of happinesse,
Foolish night-fires, womens and childrens wishes,
Chases in Arras, guilded emptinesse,
Shadows well mounted, dreams in a career,
Embroider'd lyes, nothing between two dishes;
[.i735]##{nbsp}##These are the pleasures here.
True earnest sorrows, rooted miseries,
Anguish in grain, vexations ripe and blown,
Sure-footed griefs, solid calamities,
Plain demonstrations, evident and cleare,
Fetching their proofs ev'n from the very bone;
[.i735]##{nbsp}##These are the sorrows here.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.79pt]
But[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 170 (leaf 169)
[.v,margin-top=-0.59pt]
But oh the folly of distracted men,
Who griefs in earnest, joyes in jest pursue;
Preferring, like brute beasts, a lothsome den
Before a court, ev'n that above so cleare,
Where are no sorrows, but delights more true,
[.i720]##{nbsp}##Then miseries are here!

[.poem-rule,margin-top=15.87pt]
'''

[margin-top=4.33pt]
=== ¶ The Sonne.

[.v.dropcap.p970,margin-top=4.95pt]
LEt forrain nations of their language boast,
What fine varietie each tongue affords:
I like our language, as our men and coast:
Who cannot dresse it well, want wit, not words.
How neatly doe we give one onely name
To parents issue and the sunnes bright starre!
A sonne is light and fruit; a fruitfull flame
Chasing the fathers dimnesse, carri'd farre
From the first man in th' East, to fresh and new
Western discov'ries of posteritie.
So in one word our Lords humilitie
We turn upon him in a sense most true:
[.i95]##{nbsp}##For what Christ once in humblenesse began,
[.i95]##{nbsp}##We him in glorie call, __The Sonne of Man.__

[.poem-rule,margin-top=14.39pt]
'''

[margin-top=4.01pt]
=== ¶ A true Hymne.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=10.81pt]
MY joy, my life, my crown!
My heart was meaning all the day,
[.i360]##{nbsp}##Somewhat it fain would say:
And still it runneth mutt'ring up and down
With onely this, __My joy, my life, my crown.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.95pt]
Yet[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 171 (leaf 170)
[.v,margin-top=-0.29pt]
[.i525]##{nbsp}##Yet slight not these few words:
[.i255]##{nbsp}##If truly said, they may take part
[.i450]##{nbsp}##Among the best in art.
[.i40]##{nbsp}##The finenesse which a hymne or psalme affords,
[.i40]##{nbsp}##Is, when the soul unto the lines accords.

[.v,margin-top=6.14pt]
[.i450]##{nbsp}##He who craves all the minde,
[.i170]##{nbsp}##And all the soul, and strength, and time,
[.i450]##{nbsp}##If the words onely ryme,
Justly complains, that somewhat is behinde
To make his verse, or write a hymne in kinde.

[.v,margin-top=5.93pt]
[.i450]##{nbsp}##Whereas if th' heart be moved,
[.i170]##{nbsp}##Although the verse be somewhat scant,
[.i450]##{nbsp}##God doth supply the want.
As when th' heart sayes (sighing to be approved)
__O, could I__ __love__! and stops: God writeth, __Loved.__

[.poem-rule,margin-top=11.00pt]
'''

[margin-top=5.49pt]
=== ¶ The Answer.

[.v.dropcap.p970,margin-top=4.57pt]
MY comforts drop and melt away like snow:
I shake my head, and all the thoughts and ends,
[.i130]##{nbsp}##Which my fierce youth did bandie, fall and flow
[.i130]##{nbsp}##Like leaves about me; or like summer friends,
[.i130]##{nbsp}##Flyes of estates and sunne-shine. But to all,
[.i130]##{nbsp}##Who think me eager, hot, and undertaking,
[.i130]##{nbsp}##But in my prosecutions slack and small;
[.i130]##{nbsp}##As a young exhalation, newly waking,
[.i130]##{nbsp}##Scorns his first bed of dirt, and means the sky;
[.i235]##{nbsp}##But cooling by the way, grows pursie and slow,
[.i235]##{nbsp}##And setling to a cloud, doth live and die
[.i270]##{nbsp}##In that dark state of tears: to all, that so
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Show me, and set me, I have one reply,
Which they that know the rest, know more then I.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.84pt]
¶ A[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 172 (leaf 171)
// A Dialogue-Antheme and The Water-course as the printing sets them: the
// speakers in the margin, Christian's "worst." turned up; its first
// word's capital set without its drop (a departure); the braced words,
// each on its line.
[margin-top=5.73pt]
=== ¶ A Dialogue-Antheme.

[.stage,margin-top=5.64pt]
__Christian.__{nbsp}{nbsp}{nbsp}{nbsp} __Death.__

[.v,margin-top=7.46pt]
[.i215]##Chr.##ALas, poore Death! where is thy glorie?
[.i215]##{nbsp}##Where is thy famous force, thy ancient sting?

[.v,margin-top=4.60pt]
[.i215]##Dea.##__Alas poore mortall, void of storie!__
[.i215]##{nbsp}##__Go spell and reade how I have kill'd thy King.__

[.v,margin-top=3.28pt]
[.i215]##Chr.##Poore death! and who was hurt thereby?
[.i215]##{nbsp}##Thy curse being laid on him, makes thee accurst.

[.v,margin-top=4.23pt]
[.i215]##Dea.##__Let losers talk: yet thou shalt die;__
[.i215]##{nbsp}##__These arms shall crush thee.__ [.spk]#Chr.# Spare not, do thy [.turn-up]##__worst.__##
[.i215]##{nbsp}##I shall be one day better then before:
[.i215]##{nbsp}##Thou so much worse, that thou shalt be no more.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=8pt]
'''

[margin-top=7.35pt]
=== ¶ The Water-course.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.91pt]
THou who dost dwell and linger here below,
Since the condition of this world is frail,
Where of all plants afflictions soonest grow;
If troubles overtake thee, do not wail:

[.v,margin-top=1.18pt]
[.i1715]##{nbsp}##Life?

[.v,margin-top=-5.1pt]
[.i200]##{nbsp}##For who can look for lesse, that loveth [.brace]#{#

[.v,margin-top=-4.4pt]
[.i1715]##{nbsp}##Strife?

[.v,margin-top=7.87pt]
But rather turn the pipe, and waters course
To serve thy sinnes, and furnish thee with store
Of sov'raigne tears, springing from true remorse:
That so in purenesse thou mayst him adore,

[.v,margin-top=0.66pt]
[.i1425]##{nbsp}##Salvation.

[.v,margin-top=-4.2pt]
[.i180]##{nbsp}##Who gives to man, as he sees fit, [.brace]#{#

[.v,margin-top=-5.0pt]
[.i1425]##{nbsp}##Damnation.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-5.14pt]
¶ Self-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 173 (leaf 172)
[margin-top=0.09pt]
=== ¶ Self-condemnation.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=2.33pt]
THou who condemnest Jewish hate,
For choosing Barabbas a murderer
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Before the Lord of glorie;
[.i160]##{nbsp}##Look back upon thine own estate,
Call home thine eye (that busie wanderer)
[.i400]##{nbsp}##That choice may be thy storie.

[.v,margin-top=8.17pt]
[.i160]##{nbsp}##He that doth love, and love amisse
This worlds delights before true Christian joy,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Hath made a Jewish choice:
[.i160]##{nbsp}##The world an ancient murderer is;
Thousands of souls it hath and doth destroy
[.i400]##{nbsp}##With her enchanting voice.

[.v,margin-top=7.51pt]
[.i160]##{nbsp}##He that hath made a sorrie wedding
Between his soul and gold, and hath preferr'd
[.i400]##{nbsp}##False gain before the true,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##Hath done what he condemnes in reading:
For he hath sold for money his deare Lord,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##And is a Judas-Jew.

[.v,margin-top=0.06pt]
[.i160]##{nbsp}##Thus we prevent the last great day,
And judge our selves. That light, which sin & passion
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Did before dimme and choke,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##When once those snuffes are ta'ne away,
Shines bright and cleare, ev'n unto condemnation,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##Without excuse or cloke.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=10.83pt]
'''

[margin-top=4.07pt]
=== ¶ Bitter-sweet.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=2.85pt]
AH my deare angrie Lord,
Since thou dost love, yet strike;
Cast down, yet help afford;
Sure I will do the like.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
I[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 174 (leaf 173)
[.v,margin-top=0.15pt]
I will complain, yet praise;
I will bewail, approve:
And all my sowre-sweet dayes
I will lament, and love.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=12.22pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.95pt]
=== § The Glance.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.61pt]
W[.i30]##{nbsp}##Hen first thy sweet and gracious eye
Vouchsaf'd ev'n in the midst of youth and night
To look upon me, who before did lie
[.i695]##{nbsp}##Weltring in sinne:
[.i295]##{nbsp}##I felt a sugred strange delight,
[.i295]##{nbsp}##Passing all cordials made by any art,
Bedew, embalme, and overrunne my heart,
[.i725]##{nbsp}##And take it in.

[.v.p966,margin-top=7.48pt]
[.i295]##{nbsp}##Since that time many a bitter storm
My soul hath felt, ev'n able to destroy,
Had the malicious and ill-meaning harm
[.i725]##{nbsp}##His swing and sway:
[.i295]##{nbsp}##But still thy sweet originall joy
Sprung from thine eye, did work within my soul,
And surging griefs, when they grew bold, controll,
[.i725]##{nbsp}##And got the day.

[.v.p975,margin-top=7.73pt]
[.i295]##{nbsp}##If thy first glance so powerfull be,
A mirth but open'd and seal'd up again;
What wonders shall we feel, when we shall see
[.i770]##{nbsp}##Thy full-ey'd love!
[.i295]##{nbsp}##When thou shalt look us out of pain,
And one aspect of thine spend in delight
More then a thousand sunnes disburse in light,
[.i400]##{nbsp}##In heav'n above.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.81pt]
§ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 175 (leaf 174)
[margin-top=8.20pt]
=== § The 23 Psalme.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.61pt]
THe God of love my shepherd is,
[.i80]##{nbsp}##And he that doth me feed:
While he is mine, and I am his,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##What can I want or need?

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
He leads me to the tender grasse,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Where I both feed and rest;
Then to the streams that gently passe:
[.i265]##{nbsp}##In both I have the best.

[.v,margin-top=11.78pt]
Or if I stray, he doth convert
[.i265]##{nbsp}##And bring my minde in frame:
And all this not for my desert,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##But for his holy name.

[.v,margin-top=11.42pt]
Yea, in deaths shadie black abode
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Well may I walk, not fear:
For thou art with me; and thy rod
[.i265]##{nbsp}##To guide, thy staffe to bear.

[.v,margin-top=11.56pt]
Nay, thou dost make me sit and dine,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Ev'n in my enemies sight:
My head with oyl, my cup with wine
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Runnes over day and night.

[.v,margin-top=12.04pt]
Surely thy sweet and wondrous love
[.i265]##{nbsp}##Shall measure all my dayes;
And as it never shall remove,
[.i265]##{nbsp}##So neither shall my praise.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Marie[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 176 (leaf 175)
[margin-top=0.46pt]
=== ¶ Marie Magdalene.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=3.81pt]
WHen blessed Marie wip'd her Saviours feet,
(Whose precepts she had trampled on before)
And wore them for a jewell on her head,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##Shewing his steps should be the street,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##Wherein she thenceforth evermore
With pensive humblenesse would live and tread:

[.v,margin-top=8.61pt]
She being stain'd her self, why did she strive
To make him clean, who could not be defil'd?
Why kept she not her tears for her own faults,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##And not his feet? Though we could dive
[.i160]##{nbsp}##In tears like seas, our sinnes are pil'd
Deeper then they, in words, and works, and thoughts.

[.v,margin-top=8.02pt]
Deare soul, she knew who did vouchsafe and deigne
To bear her filth; and that her sinnes did dash
Ev'n God himself: wherefore she was not loth,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##As she had brought wherewith to stain,
[.i160]##{nbsp}##So to bring in wherewith to wash:
And yet in washing one, she washed both.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=8.49pt]
'''

[margin-top=0.44pt]
=== ¶ Aaron.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=1.86pt]
H[.i370]##{nbsp}##Olinesse on the head,
[.i155]##{nbsp}##Light and perfections on the breast,
Harmonious bells below, raising the dead
[.i345]##{nbsp}##To leade them unto life and rest.
[.i560]##{nbsp}##Thus are true Aarons drest.

[.v,margin-top=8.35pt]
[.i560]##{nbsp}##Profanenesse in my head,
[.i345]##{nbsp}##Defects and darknesse in my breast,
A noise of passions ringing me for dead
[.i345]##{nbsp}##Unto a place where is no rest.
[.i560]##{nbsp}##Poore priest thus am I drest.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Onely[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 177 (leaf 176)
[.v,margin-top=-0.73pt]
[.i565]##{nbsp}##Onely another head
[.i350]##{nbsp}##I have, another heart and breast,
Another musick, making live not dead,
[.i350]##{nbsp}##Without whom I could have no rest:
[.i565]##{nbsp}##In him I am well drest.

[.v,margin-top=5.55pt]
[.i565]##{nbsp}##Christ is my onely head,
[.i350]##{nbsp}##My alone onely heart and breast,
My onely musick, striking me ev'n dead;
[.i350]##{nbsp}##That to the old man I may rest,
[.i565]##{nbsp}##And be in him new drest.

[.v,margin-top=5.85pt]
[.i565]##{nbsp}##So holy in my head,
[.i350]##{nbsp}##Perfect and light in my deare breast,
My doctrine tun'd by Christ, (who is not dead,
[.i350]##{nbsp}##But lives in me while I do rest)
[.i565]##{nbsp}##Come people; Aaron's drest.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.31pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.75pt]
=== ¶ The Odour, 2. __Cor.__ 2.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-0.28pt]
HOw sweetly doth __My Master__ sound! __My Master!__
[.i295]##{nbsp}##As Amber-greese leaves a rich sent
[.i1030]##{nbsp}##Unto the taster:
[.i480]##{nbsp}##So do these words a sweet content,
An orientall fragrancie, __My Master.__

[.v,margin-top=4.59pt]
With these all day I do perfume my minde,
[.i480]##{nbsp}##My minde ev'n thrust into them both;
[.i1030]##{nbsp}##That I might finde
[.i480]##{nbsp}##What cordials make this curious broth,
This broth of smells, that feeds and fats my minde.

[.v,margin-top=5.78pt]
__My Master,__ shall I speak? O that to thee
[.i480]##{nbsp}##__My servant__ were a little so,
[.i1030]##{nbsp}##As flesh may be;
[.i435]##{nbsp}##That these two words might creep & grow
To some degree of spicinesse to thee!

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Then[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1145]##{nbsp}##H

endif::[]

<<<

// page 178 (leaf 177)
[.v,margin-top=-0.66pt]
Then should the Pomander, which was before
[.i320]##{nbsp}##A speaking sweet, mend by reflection,
[.i945]##{nbsp}##And tell me more:
[.i320]##{nbsp}##For pardon of my imperfection
Would warm and work it sweeter then before.

[.v,margin-top=5.26pt]
For when __My Master,__ which alone is sweet,
[.i320]##{nbsp}##And ev'n in my unworthinesse pleasing,
[.i945]##{nbsp}##Shall call and meet,
[.i320]##{nbsp}##__My servant,__ as thee not displeasing,
That call is but the breathing of the sweet.

[.v,margin-top=5.77pt]
This breathing would with gains by sweetning me
[.i320]##{nbsp}##(As sweet things traffick when they meet)
[.i945]##{nbsp}##Return to thee.
[.i320]##{nbsp}##And so this new commerce and sweet
Should all my life employ, and busie me.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.50pt]
'''

[margin-top=-4.40pt]
=== ¶ The Foil.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.98pt]
I[.i495]##{nbsp}##f we could see below
The sphere of vertue, and each shining grace
[.i325]##{nbsp}##As plainly as that above doth show;
This were the better skie, the brighter place.

[.v,margin-top=5.45pt]
[.i615]##{nbsp}##God hath made starres the foil
To set off vertues; griefs to set off sinning:
[.i325]##{nbsp}##Yet in this wretched world we toil,
As if grief were not foul, nor vertue winning.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=1.64pt]
'''

[margin-top=-4.75pt]
=== ¶ The Forerunners.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-0.66pt]
THe harbingers are come. See, see their mark;
White is their colour, and behold my head.
But must they have my brain? must they dispark
Those sparkling notions, which therein were bred?
[.i310]##{nbsp}##Must dulnesse turn me to a clod?
Yet have they left me, __Thou art still my God.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Good[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 179 (leaf 178)
[.v,margin-top=2.77pt]
Good men ye be, to leave me my best room,
Ev'n all my heart, and what is lodged there:
I passe not, I, what of the rest become,
So __Thou art still my God,__ be out of fear.
[.i350]##{nbsp}##He will be pleased with that dittie;
And if I please him, I write fine and wittie.

[.v,margin-top=12.99pt]
Farewell sweet phrases, lovely metaphors.
But will ye leave me thus? when ye before
Of stews and brothels onely knew the doores,
Then did I wash you with my tears, and more,
[.i350]##{nbsp}##Brought you to Church well drest and clad;
My God must have my best, ev'n all I had.

[.v,margin-top=13.11pt]
Louely enchanting language, sugar-cane,
Hony of roses, whither wilt thou flie?
Hath some fond lover tic'd thee to thy bane?
And wilt thou leave the Church, and love a stie?
[.i350]##{nbsp}##Fie, thou wilt soil thy broider'd coat,
And hurt thy self, and him that sings the note.

[.v,margin-top=12.74pt]
Let foolish lovers, if they will love dung,
With canvas, not with arras clothe their shame:
Let follie speak in her own native tongue.
True beautie dwells on high: ours is a flame
[.i350]##{nbsp}##But borrow'd thence to light us thither.
Beautie and beauteous words should go together.

[.v,margin-top=13.14pt]
Yet if you go, I passe not; take your way:
For, __Thou art still my God,__ is all that ye
Perhaps with more embellishment can say,
Go birds of spring: let winter have his fee,
[.i380]##{nbsp}##Let a bleak palenesse chalk the doore,
So all within be livelier then before.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.25pt]
¶ The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##H 2

endif::[]

<<<

// page 180 (leaf 179)
[margin-top=5.55pt]
=== ¶ The Rose.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=6.97pt]
PResse me not to take more pleasure
[.i85]##{nbsp}##In this world of sugred lies,
And to use a larger measure
[.i310]##{nbsp}##Then my strict, yet welcome size.

[.v,margin-top=7.58pt]
First, there is no pleasure here:
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Colour'd griefs indeed there are,
Blushing woes, that look as cleare
[.i270]##{nbsp}##As if they could beautie spare.

[.v,margin-top=7.51pt]
Or if such deceits there be,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Such delights I meant to say;
There are no such things to me,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Who have pass'd my right away.

[.v,margin-top=7.66pt]
But I will not much oppose
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Unto what you now advise:
Onely take this gentle rose,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##And therein my answer lies.

[.v,margin-top=7.58pt]
What is fairer then a rose?
[.i270]##{nbsp}##What is sweeter? yet it purgeth.
Purgings enmitie disclose,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Enmitie forbearance urgeth.

[.v,margin-top=7.62pt]
If then all that worldlings prize
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Be contracted to a rose;
Sweetly there indeed it lies,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##But it biteth in the close.

[.v,margin-top=7.55pt]
So this flow'r doth judge and sentence
[.i270]##{nbsp}##Worldly joyes to be a scourge:
For they all produce repentance,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##And repentance is a purge.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
But[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 181 (leaf 180)
[.v,margin-top=-0.00pt]
But I health, not physick choose:
[.i260]##{nbsp}##Onely though I you oppose,
Say that fairly I refuse,
[.i260]##{nbsp}##For my answer is a rose.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=9.70pt]
'''

[margin-top=4.40pt]
=== ¶ Discipline.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=4.91pt]
THrow away thy rod,
Throw away thy wrath:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##O my God,
Take the gentle path.

[.v,margin-top=5.23pt]
For my hearts desire
Unto thine is bent:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##I aspire
To a full consent.

[.v,margin-top=5.30pt]
Not a word or look
I affect to own,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##But by book,
And thy book alone.

[.v,margin-top=5.22pt]
Though I fail, I weep:
Though I halt in pace,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##Yet I creep
To the throne of grace.

[.v,margin-top=5.89pt]
Then let wrath remove;
Love will do the deed:
[.i685]##{nbsp}##For with love
Stonie hearts will bleed.

[.v,margin-top=5.59pt]
Love is swift of foot;
Love's a man of warre,
[.i685]##{nbsp}##And can shoot;
And can hit from farre.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Who[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##H 3

endif::[]

<<<

// page 182 (leaf 181)
[.v,margin-top=-3.90pt]
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Who can scape his bow?
[.i30]##{nbsp}##That which wrought on thee,
[.i675]##{nbsp}##Brought thee low,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Needs must work on me.

[.v,margin-top=-0.38pt]
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Throw away thy red;
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Though man frailties hath,
[.i705]##{nbsp}##Thou art God:
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Throw away thy wrath.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.72pt]
'''

[margin-top=-0.29pt]
=== ¶ The Invitation.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=1.08pt]
COme ye hither all, whose taste
[.i595]##{nbsp}##Is your waste;
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Save your cost, and mend your fare.
[.i30]##{nbsp}##God is here prepar'd and drest,
[.i795]##{nbsp}##And the feast,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##God, in whom all dainties are.

[.v,margin-top=5.08pt]
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Come ye hither all, whom wine
[.i795]##{nbsp}##Doth define,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Naming you not to your good:
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Weep what ye have drunk amisse,
[.i795]##{nbsp}##And drink this,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Which before ye drink is bloud.

[.v,margin-top=4.81pt]
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Come ye hither all, whom pain
[.i795]##{nbsp}##Doth arraigne,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Bringing all your sinnes to sight:
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Taste and fear not: God is here
[.i795]##{nbsp}##In this cheer,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##And on sinne doth cast the fright.

[.v,margin-top=6.03pt]
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Come ye hither all, whom joy
[.i795]##{nbsp}##Doth destroy,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##While ye graze without your bounds:
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Here is joy that drowneth quite
[.i30]##{nbsp}##Your delight,
[.i30]##{nbsp}##As a floud the lower grounds.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Come[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 183 (leaf 182)
[.v,margin-top=0.41pt]
Come ye hither all, whose love
[.i795]##{nbsp}##Is your dove,
And exalts you to the skie:
Here is love, which having breath
[.i550]##{nbsp}##Ev'n in death,
After death can never die.

[.v,margin-top=6.99pt]
Lord I have invited all,
[.i795]##{nbsp}##And I shall
Still invite, still call to thee:
For it seems but just and right
[.i550]##{nbsp}##In my sight,
Where is all, there all should be.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.02pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.21pt]
=== ¶ The Banquet.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-0.91pt]
WElcome sweet and sacred cheer,
[.i465]##{nbsp}##Welcome deare;
With me, in me, live and dwell:
For thy neatnesse passeth sight,
[.i785]##{nbsp}##Thy delight
Passeth tongue to taste or tell.

[.v,margin-top=6.21pt]
O what sweetnesse from the bowl
[.i730]##{nbsp}##Fills my soul,
Such as is, and makes divine!
Is some starre (fled from the sphere)
[.i730]##{nbsp}##Melted there,
As we sugar melt in wine?

[.v,margin-top=7.06pt]
Or hath sweetnesse in the bread
[.i730]##{nbsp}##Made a head
To subdue the smell of sinne;
Flowers, and gummes, and powders giving
[.i785]##{nbsp}##All their living,
Lest the enemie should winne?

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
Doubt-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##H 4

endif::[]

<<<

// page 184 (leaf 183)
[.v,margin-top=1.99pt]
Doubtlesse, neither starre nor flower
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Hath the power
Such a sweetnesse to impart:
Onely God, who gives perfumes,
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Flesh assumes,
And with it perfumes my heart.

[.v,margin-top=12.89pt]
But as Pomanders and wood
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Still are good,
Yet being bruis'd are better sented:
God, to show how farre his love
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Could improve,
Here, as broken, is presented.

[.v,margin-top=14.91pt]
When I had forgot my birth,
[.i800]##{nbsp}##And on earth
In delights of earth was drown'd;
God took bloud, and needs would be
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Spilt with me,
And so found me on the ground.

[.v,margin-top=14.36pt]
Having rais'd me to look up,
[.i800]##{nbsp}##In a cup
Sweetly he doth meet my taste.
But I still being low and short,
[.i800]##{nbsp}##Farre from court,
Wine becomes a wing at last.

[.v,margin-top=11.01pt]
For with it alone I flie
[.i690]##{nbsp}##To the skie:
Where I wipe mine eyes, and see
What I seek, for what I sue;
[.i690]##{nbsp}##Him I view,
Who hath done so much for me.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=1.75pt]
Let[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 185 (leaf 184)
[.v,margin-top=0.63pt]
Let the wonder of this pitie
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Be my dittie,
And take up my lines and life:
Hearken under pain of death,
[.i670]##{nbsp}##Hands and breath;
Strive in this, and love the strife.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.60pt]
'''

[margin-top=-1.46pt]
=== ¶ The Posie.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.09pt]
L[.i390]##{nbsp}##Et wits contest,
And with their words and posies windows fill:
[.i580]##{nbsp}##Lesse then the least
__Of all thy mercies,__ is my posie still.

[.v,margin-top=5.63pt]
[.i580]##{nbsp}##This on my ring,
This by my picture, in my book I write;
[.i580]##{nbsp}##Whether I sing,
Or say, or dictate, this is my delight.

[.v,margin-top=5.93pt]
[.i580]##{nbsp}##Invention rest,
Comparisons go play, wit use thy will:
[.i580]##{nbsp}##Lesse then the least
__Of all Gods mercies,__ is my posie still.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=4.19pt]
'''

[margin-top=-2.33pt]
=== ¶ A Parodie.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-0.02pt]
SOuls joy, when thou art gone,
[.i300]##{nbsp}##And I alone,
[.i490]##{nbsp}##Which cannot be,
Because thou dost abide with me,
[.i200]##{nbsp}##And I depend on thee;

[.v,margin-top=5.77pt]
[.i200]##{nbsp}##Yet when thou dost suppresse
[.i490]##{nbsp}##The cheerfulnesse
[.i490]##{nbsp}##Of thy abode,
And in my powers not stirre abroad,
[.i200]##{nbsp}##But leave me to my load:

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
O[.i15]##{nbsp}##

[.foot-sig,margin-top=-9.62pt]
[.i1110]##{nbsp}##H 5

endif::[]

<<<

// page 186 (leaf 185)
[.v,margin-top=-0.07pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##O what a damp and shade
[.i485]##{nbsp}##Doth me invade!
[.i485]##{nbsp}##No stormie night
Can so afflict or so affright,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##As thy eclipsed light.

[.v,margin-top=11.30pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Ah Lord! do not withdraw,
[.i485]##{nbsp}##Lest want of aw
[.i485]##{nbsp}##Make Sinne appeare;
And when thou dost but shine lesse cleare,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Say, that thou art not here.

[.v,margin-top=11.67pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##And then what life I have,
[.i485]##{nbsp}##While Sinne doth rave,
[.i485]##{nbsp}##And falsly boast,
That I may seek, but thou art lost;
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Thou and alone thou know'st.

[.v,margin-top=11.64pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##O what a deadly cold
[.i485]##{nbsp}##Doth me infold!
[.i485]##{nbsp}##I half beleeve,
That Sinne sayes true: but while I grieve,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Thou com'st and dost relieve.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=7.07pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.64pt]
=== ¶ The Elixer.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=-0.98pt]
TEach me, my God and King,
In all things thee to see,
And what I do in any thing,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##To do it as for thee:

[.v,margin-top=5.96pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Not rudely, as a beast,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##To runne into an action;
But still to make thee prepossest,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##And give it his perfection.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
A man[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 187 (leaf 186)
[.v,margin-top=-1.18pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##A man that looks on glasse,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##On it may stay his eye;
Or if he pleaseth, through it passe,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##And then the heav'n espie.

[.v,margin-top=13.15pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##All may of thee partake:
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Nothing can be so mean,
Which with his tincture (for thy sake)
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Will not grow bright and clean.

[.v,margin-top=13.26pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##A servant with this clause
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Makes drudgerie divine:
Who sweeps a room, as for thy laws,
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Makes that and th' action fine.

[.v,margin-top=13.04pt]
[.i205]##{nbsp}##This is the famous stone
[.i205]##{nbsp}##That turneth all to gold:
For that which God doth touch and own
[.i205]##{nbsp}##Cannot for lesse be told.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=3.43pt]
'''

[margin-top=-1.20pt]
=== ¶ A Wreath.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.72pt]
A Wreathed garland of deserved praise,
Of praise deserved, unto thee I give,
I give to thee, who knowest all my wayes,
My crooked winding wayes, wherein I live,
Wherein I die, not live: for life is straight,
Straight as a line, and ever tends to thee,
To thee, who art more farre above deceit,
Then deceit seems above simplicitie.
Give me simplicitie, that I may live,
So live and like, that I may know thy wayes,
Know them and practise them: then shall I give
For this poore wreath, give thee a crown of praise.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-1.80pt]
¶ Death.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 188 (leaf 187)
[margin-top=8.61pt]
=== ¶ Death.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=12.83pt]
DEath, thou wast once an uncouth hideous thing,
[.i745]##{nbsp}##Nothing but bones,
[.i270]##{nbsp}##The sad effect of sadder grones:
Thy mouth was open, but thou couldst not sing.

[.v,margin-top=11.60pt]
[.i40]##{nbsp}##For we consider'd thee as at some six
[.i955]##{nbsp}##Or ten yeares hence,
[.i355]##{nbsp}##After the losse of life and sense,
[.i40]##{nbsp}##Flesh being turn'd to dust, and bones to sticks.

[.v,margin-top=11.60pt]
[.i40]##{nbsp}##We lookt on this side of thee, shooting short;
[.i1015]##{nbsp}##Where we did finde
[.i355]##{nbsp}##The shells of fledge souls left behinde,
[.i40]##{nbsp}##Dry dust, which sheds no tears, but may extort.

[.v,margin-top=11.64pt]
[.i40]##{nbsp}##But since our Saviours death did put some bloud
[.i1015]##{nbsp}##Into thy face;
[.i355]##{nbsp}##Thou art grown fair and full of grace,
[.i40]##{nbsp}##Much in request, much sought for, as a good.

[.v,margin-top=11.93pt]
[.i40]##{nbsp}##For we do now behold thee gay and glad,
[.i850]##{nbsp}##As at dooms-day;
[.i355]##{nbsp}##When souls shall wear their new aray,
[.i40]##{nbsp}##And all thy bones with beautie shall be clad.

[.v,margin-top=12.15pt]
[.i40]##{nbsp}##Therefore we can go die as sleep, and trust
[.i955]##{nbsp}##Half that we have
[.i300]##{nbsp}##Unto an honest faithfull grave;
Making our pillows either down, or dust.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.88pt]
¶ Dooms-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 189 (leaf 188)
[margin-top=1.75pt]
=== ¶ Dooms-day.

[.v,margin-top=7.75pt]
[.i745]##{nbsp}##COme away,
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Make no delay.
Summon all the dust to rise,
Till it stirre, and rubbe the eyes;
While this member jogs the other,
Each one whispring, __Live you brother?__

[.v,margin-top=5.51pt]
[.i745]##{nbsp}##Come away,
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Make this the day.
Dust, alas, no musick feels,
But thy trumpet: then it kneels,
As peculiar notes and strains
Cure Tarantulaes raging pains.

[.v,margin-top=5.89pt]
[.i745]##{nbsp}##Come away,
[.i395]##{nbsp}##O make no stay!
Let the graves make their confession,
Lest at length they plead possession:
Fleshes stubbornnesse may have
Read that lesson to the grave.

[.v,margin-top=7.73pt]
[.i745]##{nbsp}##Come away,
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Thy flock doth stray.
Some to windes their bodie lend,
And in them may drown a friend:
Some in noisome vapours grow
To a plague and publick wo.

[.v,margin-top=8.16pt]
[.i745]##{nbsp}##Come away,
[.i395]##{nbsp}##Help our decay.
Man is out of order hurl'd,
Parcel'd out to all the world.
Lord, thy broken consort raise,
And the musick shall be praise.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=4.78pt]
¶ Judge-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 190 (leaf 189)
[margin-top=-1.16pt]
=== ¶ Judgement.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=2.18pt]
ALmightie Judge, how shall poore wretches brook
[.i750]##{nbsp}##Thy dreadfull look,
Able a heart of iron to appall,
[.i935]##{nbsp}##When thou shalt call
[.i290]##{nbsp}##For ev'ry mans peculiar book?

[.v,margin-top=7.07pt]
What others mean to do, I know not well;
[.i935]##{nbsp}##Yet I heare tell,
That some will turn thee to some leaves therein
[.i935]##{nbsp}##So void of sinne,
[.i290]##{nbsp}##That they in merit shall excell.

[.v,margin-top=7.36pt]
But I resolve, when thou shalt call for mine,
[.i935]##{nbsp}##That to decline,
And thrust a Testament into thy hand:
[.i935]##{nbsp}##Let that be scann'd.
[.i290]##{nbsp}##There thou shalt finde my faults are thine.

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.53pt]
'''

[margin-top=3.33pt]
=== ¶ Heaven.

[.v.dropcap.p964,margin-top=-1.07pt]
O Who will show me those delights on high?
__Echo. I.__
Thou Echo, thou art mortall, all men know.
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. No.__
Wert thou not born among the trees and leaves?
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Leaves.__
And are there any leaves, that still abide?
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Bide.__
What leaves are they? impart the matter wholly.
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Holy.__
Are holy leaves the Echo then of blisse?
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Yes.__
Then tell me, what is that supreme delight?
[.i940]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Light.__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-0.23pt]
Light[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 191 (leaf 190)
[.v,margin-top=-0.40pt]
Light to the minde: what shall the will enjoy?
[.i875]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Ioy.__
But are there eares and businesse with the pleasure?
[.i875]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Leisure.__
Light, joy, and leisure; but shall they persever?
[.i875]##{nbsp}##__Echo. Ever.__

[.poem-rule,margin-top=6.13pt]
'''

[margin-top=1.02pt]
=== ¶ Love.

[.v.dropcap,margin-top=5.76pt]
LOve bade me welcome: yet my soul drew back,
[.i475]##{nbsp}##Guiltie of dust and sinne.
But quick-ey'd Love, observing me grow slack
[.i665]##{nbsp}##From my first entrance in,
Drew nearer to me, sweetly questioning,
[.i665]##{nbsp}##If I lack'd any thing.

[.v,margin-top=5.74pt]
A guest, I answer'd, worthy to be here:
[.i665]##{nbsp}##Love said, you shall be he.
I the unkinde, ungratefull? Ah my deare,
[.i665]##{nbsp}##I cannot look on thee.
Love took my hand, and smiling did reply,
[.i665]##{nbsp}##Who made the eyes but I?

[.v,margin-top=7.65pt]
Truth Lord, but I have marr'd them: let my shame
[.i665]##{nbsp}##Go where it doth deserve.
And know you not, sayes Love, who bore the blame?
[.i665]##{nbsp}##My deare, then I will serve.
You must sit down, sayes Love, and taste my meat:
[.i665]##{nbsp}##So I did sit and eat.

[.finis,margin-top=12.40pt]
FINIS.

[.gloria,margin-top=11.52pt]
Glorie be to God on high, and on earth
peace, good will towards men.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-4.34pt]
The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 192 (leaf 191)
// The Church Militant's first page as the printing sets it: a band of
// ornament over its title; its first word's capital a factotum, a woodcut
// six lines high, the lines beside it set in past it and broken where the
// printing breaks them, their ends set in under them.
ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=2.91pt]
image::temple-band-militant.png[pdfwidth=182.9pt]
endif::[]

[#church-militant,running-title="The Church Militant.",margin-top=8.46pt]
== The Church Militant.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.factotum,margin-top=8.20pt]
image::temple-factotum-a.png[A,pdfwidth=53.29pt,align=left]

[.v.p966,margin-top=-51.66pt]
[.i545]##{nbsp}##Lmightie Lord, who from thy glorious
[.i735]##{nbsp}##throne
[.i545]##{nbsp}##Seest and rulest all things ev'n as one:
[.i555]##{nbsp}##The smallest ant or atome knows thy
[.i725]##{nbsp}##power,
[.i535]##{nbsp}##Known also to each minute of an houre:
Much more do Common-weals acknowledge thee,
And wrap their policies in thy decree,
Complying with thy counsels, doing nought
Which doth not meet with an eternall thought.
But above all, thy Church and Spouse doth prove
Not the decrees of power, but bands of love.
Early didst thou arise to plant this vine,
Which might the more indeare it to be thine.
Spices come from the East; so did thy Spouse,
Trimme as the light, sweet as the laden boughs
Of __Noahs__ shadie vine, chaste as the dove;
Prepar'd and fitted to receive thy love,
The course was westward, that the sunne might light
As well our understanding as our sight.
Where th' Ark did rest, there __Abraham__ began
To bring the other Ark from __Canaan.__
__Moses__ pursu'd this: but King __Solomon__
Finish'd and fixt the old religion.
When it grew loose, the Jews did hope in vain
By nailing Christ to fasten it again.
But to the Gentiles he bore crosse and all,
Rending with earthquakes the partition-wall:
Onely whereas the Ark in glorie shone,
Now with the crosse, as with a staffe, alone,
Religion, like a pilgrime, westward bent,
endif::[]
ifndef::backend-pdf[]
[.v]
ALmightie Lord, who from thy glorious throne
Seest and rulest all things ev'n as one:
The smallest ant or atome knows thy power,
Known also to each minute of an houre:
Much more do Common-weals acknowledge thee,
And wrap their policies in thy decree,
Complying with thy counsels, doing nought
Which doth not meet with an eternall thought.
But above all, thy Church and Spouse doth prove
Not the decrees of power, but bands of love.
Early didst thou arise to plant this vine,
Which might the more indeare it to be thine.
Spices come from the East; so did thy Spouse,
Trimme as the light, sweet as the laden boughs
Of __Noahs__ shadie vine, chaste as the dove;
Prepar'd and fitted to receive thy love,
The course was westward, that the sunne might light
As well our understanding as our sight.
Where th' Ark did rest, there __Abraham__ began
To bring the other Ark from __Canaan.__
__Moses__ pursu'd this: but King __Solomon__
Finish'd and fixt the old religion.
When it grew loose, the Jews did hope in vain
By nailing Christ to fasten it again.
But to the Gentiles he bore crosse and all,
Rending with earthquakes the partition-wall:
Onely whereas the Ark in glorie shone,
Now with the crosse, as with a staffe, alone,
Religion, like a pilgrime, westward bent,
endif::[]

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.04pt]
Knock-[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 193 (leaf 192)
[.v.p955,margin-top=-0.70pt]
Knocking at all doores, ever as she went.
Yet as the sunne, though forward be his flight,
Listens behinde him, and allows some light,
Till all depart: so went the Church her way,
Letting, while one foot stept, the other stay
Among the eastern nations for a time,
Till both removed to the western clime.
To __Egypt__ first she came, where they did prove
Wonders of anger once, but now of love.
The ten Commandments there did flourish more
Then the ten bitter plagues had done before.
Holy __Macarius__ and great __Anthonie__
Made __Pharaoh Moses,__ changing th' historie.
Goshen was darknesse, __Egypt__ full of lights,
__Nilus__ for monsters brought forth Israelites.
Such power hath mightie Baptisme to produce
For things misshapen, things of highest use.
__How deare to me, O God, thy counsels are!__
[.i750]##{nbsp}##__Who may with thee compare?__
Religion thence fled into __Greece,__ where arts
Gave her the highest place in all mens hearts.
Learning was pos'd, Philosophie was set,
Sophisters taken in a fishers net.
__Plato__ and __Aristotle__ were at a losse,
And wheel'd about again to spell __Christ-Crosse.__
Prayers chas'd syllogismes into their den,
And __Ergo__ was trasform'd into __Amen.__
Though __Greece__ took horse as soon as __Egypt__ did;
And __Rome__ as both; yet __Egypt__ faster rid,
And spent her period and prefixed time
Before the other. __Greece__ being past her prime,
Religion went to __Rome,__ subduing those,
Who, that they might subdue, made all their foes.
The Warrier his deere skarres no more resounds,
But seems to yeeld Christ hath the greater wounds,
Wounds willingly endur'd to work his blisse,
Who by an ambush lost his Paradise,

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.91pt]
The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 194 (leaf 193)
[.v.p953,margin-top=-0.88pt]
The great heart stoops, and taketh from the dust
A sad repentance, not the spoils of lust:
Quitting his spear, lest it should pierce again
Him in his members, who for him was slain.
The Shepherds hook grew to a scepter here,
Giving new names and numbers to the yeare.
But th' Empire dwelt in __Greece,__ to comfort them
Who were cut short in __Alexanders__ stemme.
In both of these Prowesse and Arts did tame
And tune mens hearts against the Gospel came.
Which using, and not fearing skill in th' one,
Or strength in th' other, did erect her throne.
Many a rent and struggling th' Empire knew,
(As dying things are wont) untill it flew
At length to __Germanie,__ still westward bending,
And there the Churches festivall attending:
That as before Empire and Arts made way,
(For no lesse Harbingers would serve then they)
So they might still, and point us out the place
Where first the Church should raise her down-cast face,
Strength levels grounds, Arts makes a garden there;
Then showres Religion, and makes all to bear.
__Spain__ in the Empire shar'd with __Germanie,__
But __England__ in the higher victorie:
Giving the Church a crown to keep her state,
And not go lesse then she had done of late.
__Constantines__ British line meant this of old,
And did this mysterie wrap up and fold
Within a sheet of paper, which was rent
From times great Chronicle, and hither sent.
Thus both the Church and Sunne together ran
Unto the farthest old meridian.
__How deare to me, O God, thy counsels are!__
__Who may with thee compare?__
Much about one and the same time and place,
Both where and when the Church began her race,

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.92pt]
Sinne[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 195 (leaf 194)
[.v.p954,margin-top=-1.14pt]
Sinne did set out of Eastern __Babylon,__
And travell'd westward also: journeying on
He chid the Church away, where e're he came,
Breaking her peace, and tainting her good name.
At first he got to __Egypt,__ and did sow
Gardens of gods, which ev'ry yeare did grow,
Fresh and fine deities. They were at great cost,
Who for a god clearely a sallet lost.
Ah, what a thing is man devoid of grace,
Adoring garlick with an humble face,
Begging his food of that which he may ear,
Starving the while he worshippeth his meat!
Who makes a root his god, how low is he,
If God and man be sever'd infinitely!
What wretchednesse can give him any room,
Whose house is foul, while he adores his broom?
None will beleeve this now, though money be
In us the same transplanted foolerie.
Thus Sinne in __Egypt__ sneaked for a while;
His highest was an ox or crocodile,
And such poore game. Thence he to __Greece__ doth passe,
And being craftier much then Goodnesse was,
He left behinde him garrisons of sinnes
To make good that which ev'ry day he winnes.
Here Sinne took heart, and for a garden-bed
Rich shrines and oracles he purchased:
He grew a gallant, and would needs foretell
As well what should befall, as what befell.
Nay, he became a poet, and would serve
His pills of sublimate in that conserve.
The world came both with hands and purses full
To this great lotterie, and all would pull.
But all was glorious cheating, brave deceit,
Where some poore truths were shuffled for a bait
To credit him, and to discredit those
Who after him should braver truths disclose.

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-0.84pt]
From[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 196 (leaf 195)
[.v.p954,margin-top=-0.91pt]
From __Greece__ he went to __Rome:__ and as before
He was a God, now he's an Emperour.
__Nero__ and others lodg'd him bravely there,
Put him in trust to rule the Romane sphere,
Glorie was his chief instrument of old:
Pleasure succeeded straight, when that grew cold.
Which soon was blown to such a mighty flame,
That though our Saviour did destroy the game,
Disparking oracles, and all their treasure,
Setting affliction to encounter pleasure;
Yet did a rogue with hope of carnall joy
Cheat the most subtill nations. Who so coy,
So trimme, as __Greece__ and __Egypt?__ yet their hearts
Are given over, for their curious arts,
To such Mahometan stupidities,
As the old heathen would deem prodigies.
__How deare to me, O God, thy counsels are!__
__Who may with thee compare?__
Onely the West and __Rome__ do keep them free
From this contagious infidelitie.
And this is all the Rock, whereof they boast,
As __Rome__ will one day finde unto her cost.
Sinne being not able to extirpate quite
The Churches here, bravely resolv'd one night
To be a Church-man too, and wear a Mitre:
The old debauched ruffian would turn writer.
I saw him in his studie, where he sate
Busie in controversies sprung of late.
A gown and pen became him wondrous well:
His grave aspect had more of heav'n then hell:
Onely there was a handsome picture by,
To which he lent a corner of his eye.
As Sinne in __Greece__ a Prophet was before;
And in old __Rome__ a mightie Emperour;
So now being Priest he plainly did professe
To make a jest of Christs three offices:

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=-0.63pt]
The[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 197 (leaf 196)
[.v.p959,margin-top=-0.21pt]
The rather since his scatter'd jugglings were
United now in one both time and sphere.
From __Egypt__ he took pettie deities,
From __Greece__ oracular infallibities,
And from old __Rome__ the libertie of pleasure,
By free dispensings of the Churches treasure.
Then in memoriall of his ancient throne
He did surname his palace, __Babylon.__
Yet that he might the better gain all nations,
And make that name good by their transmigrations;
From all these places, but at divers times,
He took fine vizards to conceal his crimes:
From __Egypt__ Anchorisme and retirednesse,
Learning from __Greece,__ from old __Rome__ statelinesse:
And blending these he carri'd all mens eyes,
While Truth sat by, counting his victories:
Whereby he grew apace and scorn'd to use
Such force as once did captivate the Jews;
But did bewitch, and finely work each nation
Into a voluntarie transmigration.
All poste to __Rome:__ Princes submit their necks
Either t' his publick foot or private tricks.
It did not fit his gravitie to stirre,
Nor his long journey, nor his gout and furre.
Therefore he sent out able ministers,
Statesmen within, without doores cloisterers:
Who without spear, or sword, or other drumme
Then what was in their tongue, did overcome;
And having conquer'd, did so strangely rule,
That the whole world did seem but the Popes mule.
As new and old __Rome__ did one Empire twist;
So both together are one Antichrist,
Yet with two faces, as their __Ianus__ was;
Being in this their old crackt looking-grasse.
__How deare to me, O God, thy counsels are!__
__Who may with thee compare?__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.69pt]
Thus[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 198 (leaf 197)
[.v.p957,margin-top=-0.86pt]
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Thus Sinne triumphs in Western __Babylon__;
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Yet not as Sinne, but as Religion.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Of his two thrones he made the latter best,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##And to defray his journey from the east.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Old and new __Babylon__ are to hell and night,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##As is the moon and sunne to heav'n and light.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##When th' one did set, the other did take place,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Confronting equally the law and grace.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##They are hells land-marks, Satans double crest:
[.i35]##{nbsp}##They are Sinnes nipples, feeding th' east and west.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##But as in vice the copie still exceeds
[.i35]##{nbsp}##The pattern, but not so in vertuous deeds;
[.i35]##{nbsp}##So though Sinne made his latter seat the better,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##The latter Church is to the first a debter.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##The second Temple could not reach the first:
[.i35]##{nbsp}##And the late reformation never durst
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Compare with ancient times and purer yeares;
[.i35]##{nbsp}##But in the Jews and us deserveth tears.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Nay, it shall ev'ry yeare decrease and fade;
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Till such a darknesse do the world invade
[.i35]##{nbsp}##At Christs last coming, as his first did finde:
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Yet must there such proportions be assign'd
[.i35]##{nbsp}##To these diminishings, as is between
[.i35]##{nbsp}##The spacious world and __Jurie__ to be seen.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Religion stands on tip-toe in our land,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Readie to passe to the __American__ strand.
[.i35]##{nbsp}##When height of malice, and prodigious lusts,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Impudent sinning, witchcrafts, and distrusts
[.i35]##{nbsp}##(The marks of future bane) shall fill our cup
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Unto the brimme, and make our measure up:
[.i35]##{nbsp}##When __Sein__ shall swallow __Tiber,__ and the __Thames__
[.i35]##{nbsp}##By letting in them both, pollutes her streams:
[.i35]##{nbsp}##When __Italie__ of us shall have her will,
[.i35]##{nbsp}##And all her calender of sinnes fulfill;
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Whereby one may foretell, what sinnes next yeare
[.i35]##{nbsp}##Shall both in __France__ and __England__ domineer:

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.15pt]
Then[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 199 (leaf 198)
[.v.p957,margin-top=0.10pt]
Then shall Religion to __America__ flee:
They have their times of Gospel, ev'n as we.
My God, thou dost prepare for them a way
By carrying first their gold from them away:
For gold and grace did never yet agree:
Religion alwaies sides with povertie.
We think we rob them, but we think amisse:
We are more poore, and they more rich by this.
Thou wilt revenge their quarrell, making grace
To pay our debts, and leave our ancient place
To go to them, while that which now their nation
But lends to us, shall be our desolation.
Yet as the Church shall thither westward flie,
So Sinne shall trace and dog her instantly:
They have their period also and set times
Both for their vertuous actions and their crimes.
And where of old the Empire and the Arts
Usher'd the Gospel ever in mens hearts,
__Spain__ hath done one; when Arts perform the other,
[.i75]##{nbsp}##The Church shall come, & Sinne the Church shall smo[.turn-down]##ther:##
[.i75]##{nbsp}##That when they haue accomplished the round,
And met in th' east their first and ancient sound,
Judgement may meet them both & search them round
Thus do both lights, as well in Church as Sunne,
Light one another, and together runne.
Thus also Sinne and Darknesse follow still
The Church and Sunne with all their power and skill.
But as the Sunne still goes both west and east;
So also did the Church by going west
Still eastward go; because it drew more neare
To time and place, where judgement shall appeare.
__How deare to me, O God, thy counsels are!__
[.i830]##{nbsp}##__Who may with thee compare?__

ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.foot-cw,margin-top=0.90pt]
¶ L'Envoy.[.i15]##{nbsp}##

endif::[]

<<<

// page 200 (leaf 199)
ifdef::backend-pdf[]
[.band,margin-top=0.18pt]
image::temple-band-envoy.png[pdfwidth=188.7pt]
endif::[]

[margin-top=12.37pt]
=== ¶ L'Envoy.

[.v.dropcap.p956,margin-top=11.28pt]
KIng of glorie, King of Peace,
With the one make warre to cease;
With the other blesse thy sheep,
Thee to love, in thee to sleep.
Let not Sinne devoure thy fold,
Bragging that thy bloud is cold,
That thy death is also dead,
While his conquests daily spread;
That thy flesh hath lost his food,
And thy Crosse is common wood.
Choke him, let him say no more,
But reserve his breath in store,
Till thy conquests and his fall
Make his sighs to use it all,
And then bargain with the winde
To discharge what is behinde.

[.v,margin-top=28.80pt]
[.i140]##{nbsp}##__Blessed be God alone,__
[.i140]##{nbsp}##__Thrice blessed Three in One.__

[.finis,margin-top=32.26pt]
FINIS.
